# Wiki Index

Canonical list of all wiki pages, grouped by domain (the primary retrieval axis).
Regenerate after every ingest. `data/wiki/private/` is deliberately excluded.

**1863 pages** across 17 domains.

## Programming

- [[AutoGym Blueprint-First Verifiable Gyms]] — AutoGym generates agent training environments from a blueprint that fixes the solution, requirements and verification before any environment exists, with ground truth computed by execution; moving only difficulty parameters raised a hard-task share from 10% to 39% (self-reported).
- [[B-Trees vs LSM-Trees]] — The two ways stores keep an index: B-trees update fixed pages in place behind a write-ahead log, LSM-trees append immutable sorted segments and merge them; trade-offs in write cost, read cost and tail latency.
- [[Clean Code: Error Handling and Boundaries]] — Feathers and Grenning's chapters in Martin's Clean Code: keep error handling out of the main logic, wrap third-party code behind an interface you control, and pin outside behaviour with learning tests.
- [[Clean Code: Names, Functions and Comments]] — Martin's guidance on how to name things, size functions and when to write comments, with the Java-era caveats and the fact that it rests on one author's experience rather than measurement.
- [[Clean Code: Objects, Classes and Systems]] — Martin and his guest authors on design above the function: objects versus data structures, size classes by responsibility, and keep construction separate from use in systems.
- [[Clean Code: Tests and Refactoring Discipline]] — Martin's guidance that tests are held to the same cleanliness as production code and that messy first drafts are cleaned in many tiny test-protected steps, with the case studies (Args, ComparisonCompactor, SerialDate) as single-author worked examples.
- [[Clustering Algorithms: k-means, Agglomerative and DBSCAN]] — k-means needs round similar clusters, agglomerative gives a dendrogram without prediction, DBSCAN finds arbitrary shapes and noise; with no ground truth, inspect the clusters.
- [[Expand, Migrate, Contract]] — A rename, split or type change cannot be one statement because old and new code run together during a rollout; add the new shape, dual write, backfill, verify, move readers, and drop the old shape last, since contract is the one step with no rollback.
- [[Flashcard Schedulers: Leitner, SM-2 and FSRS]] — Leitner boxes and SM-2 need no review history, while FSRS tracks per-card memory state, fits parameters per user or preset and beats half-life regression at predicting recall on its own project's benchmark; prediction is not learning, and no controlled comparison of learning outcomes was found, so start simple.
- [[Git Branch Triage]] — Deciding what to do with in-flight work before switching context — the branch health check that separates merged from unmerged commits, the WIP-branch-versus-stash choice, and reading a stash diff before trusting it.
- [[IPython Timing and Profiling Workflow]] — IPython's magics measure speed in a fixed order (timeit, then prun, lprun, memit, mprun), and %timeit misleads on in-place operations such as sorting because repeated runs act on already-changed data.
- [[Latency Percentiles, Tail Amplification and Coordinated Omission]] — Response time is a distribution: report percentiles, merge histograms rather than averaging percentiles, expect the tail to compound across backend calls, and measure with an open-loop load generator.
- [[NumPy Vectorisation, Views and Fancy Indexing]] — NumPy replaces Python loops with whole-array operations, but slices are views while boolean and fancy indexing copy, and a few default behaviours (repeated indices, wrong-length masks, int truncation) fail without an error.
- [[Out of the Tar Pit: Essential and Accidental Complexity]] — Moseley and Marks's 2006 position paper argues that most software complexity is accidental, that mutable state is its largest cause, and proposes Avoid and Separate plus a hypothetical Functional Relational Programming architecture.
- [[Programming Curriculum]] — Course map for the three languages an AI or data engineer writes daily — Python, SQL, TypeScript — with the phase order, the hand-offs between tracks, the rules of thumb each track ends on, and where each topic's wiki page lives.
- [[Python Closures and Decorators]] — Functions are values: closures capture variables (not values) from their defining scope, a decorator is a function that wraps a function (always with `functools.wraps`), and a decorator with arguments adds one more level — the shape behind timing, retry and caching wrappers.
- [[Python Concurrency Models]] — Pick by what you're waiting on: threads (ThreadPoolExecutor) for a few dozen blocking I/O calls, asyncio for hundreds of concurrent requests or async SDKs, processes for CPU-bound pure Python — and in asyncio, never block the event loop, bound fan-out with a Semaphore, add deadlines, and retry only transient errors with backoff plus jitter.
- [[Python Data Model and Idioms]] — Names are labels on objects, not boxes — which explains aliasing, the mutable-default trap and pass-by-assignment; dunder methods map syntax onto your classes; hashability rules decide what can be a key; and a short list of idioms (unpacking, EAFP, dict merge) should be reflexive.
- [[Python Exceptions and Context Managers]] — Catch only what you can handle, chain re-raised errors with `raise … from err`, give each package one exception base class with fields callers can branch on, and put cleanup in `with`/`finally` — `@contextmanager` turns a try/finally around a `yield` into a reusable resource guard.
- [[Python Foundations Reference]] — Compact Python-for-Everybody-level reference — types and operators with their traps (floor division, truthiness, short-circuit guards), return-don't-print functions, loop patterns and their built-ins, strings and files, collections and aliasing, regex, HTTP, JSON/XML APIs, classes and sqlite3 — plus the course's rules of thumb.
- [[Python Generators and Lazy Iteration]] — Iterators are single-use and generators run only on demand, so chained generator pipelines process arbitrarily large inputs in constant memory — but a second pass over an exhausted iterator silently yields nothing, and a generator's open `with` block lives until it is consumed.
- [[Python Packaging and Tooling]] — A modern Python project is a `pyproject.toml`, a `src/` layout and `tests/`, run with uv (envs, deps, lockfile), ruff (lint + format) and pytest (plain asserts, parametrize, fixtures) — and most import bugs come from `sys.path` and stdlib-shadowing filenames, fixed by installing the project rather than editing paths.
- [[Python Type Hints and Dataclasses]] — Type hints are checked by tools, not Python: type every signature (loose in, specific out, `X | None` to force None handling, `object` over `Any`), use Protocols for checker-understood duck typing, frozen dataclasses for trusted internal data, and Pydantic to validate untrusted data at every boundary.
- [[PyTorch Training Loop Silent Bugs]] — A checklist of training-loop mistakes that raise no error but cost accuracy: softmax before a cross-entropy that already applies it, forgetting zero_grad, confusing eval() with no_grad(), the dropout p convention, and distributed-sampler details.
- [[Quorums, Version Vectors and Last-Write-Wins]] — Leaderless replication reads and writes several replicas with w + r > n so sets overlap; concurrent writes are resolved by version vectors that keep siblings, or by last-write-wins that silently drops acknowledged writes.
- [[Repo Weight Triage — GC Before LFS]] — When a repo feels too heavy, measure before choosing a fix. Loose objects are reclaimable by git gc, duplicate blobs cost working tree rather than history, and re-fetchable files need a manifest rather than storage. Sequence: gc, dedupe, untrack media, then decide gitignore+manifest. Avoid submodules and LFS at personal-repo scale.
- [[Runtime Validation with Zod]] — Types are erased, so env vars, JSON bodies, files, tool inputs and LLM output are really `unknown`: parse them once at the boundary with a zod schema that yields both the runtime check and the static type (`z.infer`), then trust the typed value everywhere after — parse, don't validate.
- [[Spaced Repetition and Self-Quizzing From a Knowledge Base]] — Retrieving material beats rereading it at a delay but not immediately, and the best gap between sessions grows with how long you need to keep it; this wiki infers that a KB should ask recall questions and grade them later, with no credit for how a quiz scores straight after reading.
- [[SQL from Python]] — SQL does the set operations and Python orchestrates: sqlite3 connection/cursor mechanics (the `with` block commits but doesn't close), parameters instead of f-strings with allow-lists for identifiers, DuckDB for SQL over files and DataFrames, and filter-in-SQL/reshape-in-pandas as the division of labour.
- [[SQL Indexes and Query Plans]] — A B-tree index turns a full scan into a log-n lookup but every write pays for it; read EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN for SCAN vs SEARCH, order composite indexes equality-first/range-last, keep predicates sargable (no function on the indexed column), and know when indexes don't help — including columnar engines that use zone maps instead.
- [[SQL Join Patterns]] — Choosing inner, left, self, anti and cross joins by what should happen to unmatched rows — and the two silent-wrong-answer traps: a WHERE filter on a LEFT JOIN's right table turning it back into an inner join, and fan-out when the join key isn't unique.
- [[SQL NULL and Three-Valued Logic]] — NULL means unknown, so any comparison with it is unknown and WHERE drops it — which silently breaks `!=` filters, `= NULL`, and `NOT IN` over a nullable subquery; plus the related expression traps: half-open date ranges, integer division, and dialect-specific string/date functions.
- [[SQL Query Fundamentals]] — Core SELECT/WHERE/ORDER BY/CASE/LIMIT and GROUP BY/HAVING mechanics, organised around the logical evaluation order (FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → ORDER BY → LIMIT) that explains most alias and filtering errors, plus the rules of thumb that prevent silent wrong answers.
- [[SQL Schema Design and DDL]] — A schema makes promises about its data and constraints let the database keep them: the four data-hygiene rules behind normal forms, PRIMARY KEY/UNIQUE/CHECK/FOREIGN KEY semantics (and SQLite's off-by-default foreign keys), safe UPDATE/DELETE, ON CONFLICT upserts for idempotent loads, atomic transactions, and views vs materialised views.
- [[SQL Subqueries and CTEs]] — A query is a table, so it can go anywhere SQL expects a table or value: scalar, correlated and derived-table subqueries, EXISTS vs IN (and why NOT IN breaks on NULL), CTEs for readable multi-step queries, and recursive CTEs for hierarchies and gap-free date series.
- [[SQL Window Functions]] — A window function computes over a group but keeps every row — the tool for top-N per group, dedup-latest, running totals, moving averages, lag/lead comparisons and share-of-total; it runs after GROUP BY so it must be filtered from an outer CTE, and its default RANGE frame silently groups ties.
- [[Tidy Data and Long-Wide Reshaping]] — Ask what one row means; tidy data puts each variable in a column and each observation in a row, and melt, pivot, stack and unstack move between long and wide layouts.
- [[TypeScript any Escapes]] — The three ways out of an `any` — a real type, `unknown` with narrowing, or a generic — plus why types files are the highest-leverage place to fix them and how exhaustive-deps catches stale closures rather than style violations.
- [[TypeScript Async Patterns]] — A JS promise starts running when it is created (unlike an asyncio coroutine), so the choice is sequential vs Promise.all parallel; Promise.race does not cancel the loser, forEach does not await, and an unbounded Promise.all over thousands of requests needs a concurrency limit — pass an AbortSignal down to cancel real work.
- [[TypeScript Discriminated Unions and Narrowing]] — Model 'one of these' as a union with a literal `kind` field, narrow by control flow, and end the `switch` with `const x: never = s` so adding a member without handling it is a compile error; type predicates package a check but are trusted, not verified.
- [[TypeScript for AI Apps]] — Treat an LLM API as an untyped, slow, occasionally failing remote call: narrow content blocks by `type` (never assume `content[0]` is text), drive the tool loop from an exhaustive `stop_reason` switch, stream long requests, validate tool inputs and structured output with zod, and keep parsing and loop logic pure so it tests offline.
- [[TypeScript for Python Developers]] — TypeScript is JavaScript with a checker that runs and then disappears, so learn the runtime traps first (`==` coercion, string-comparing `sort()`, truthy `[]`, reference equality), then annotate boundaries, prefer `unknown` over `any`, and turn on `strict` from day one.
- [[TypeScript Generics and Type Transformations]] — Generics keep the caller's type through a function (a type parameter should appear at least twice); keyof/T[K], overloads, utility, mapped, conditional and template-literal types derive new types from one source — and type-level code is real code, so stop before infer goes three levels deep.
- [[TypeScript Modules and Tooling]] — Most 'TypeScript is hard' pain is module and config pain: use ESM with named exports and explicit `import type`, one strict tsconfig (nodenext, verbatimModuleSyntax, erasableSyntaxOnly, noEmit), run with Node type stripping and check with tsc separately, and use .d.ts files and module augmentation for untyped or extended libraries.

## Data Analytics

- [[A/B Test Lifecycle and Interval Decisions]] — A sound A/B test fixes the metric, the minimum detectable effect and the sample before launch, randomises and analyses at the same unit, checks the split, tests once, and decides by comparing the confidence interval with the effect worth shipping rather than reading the p-value.
- [[Anscombe's Quartet: Plot Before Summarising]] — Four datasets with identical means, variance, correlation and fitted line look completely different, so summary statistics alone cannot replace a plot.
- [[Approximate Bayesian Computation with Summary Statistics]] — When the likelihood of a whole dataset is too tiny and too slow to compute, score each hypothesis by the likelihood of summary statistics instead; the choice of statistic then becomes a modelling decision that can reverse the answer.
- [[Attribution Credit vs Causal Share]] — Attribution divides each sale's credit among the channels on its path and must hand out every conversion; last touch and Shapley both overcredit channels that reach already-ready buyers, so credit is accounting, not cause.
- [[Backdoor and Front-Door Criteria]] — A set Z satisfies the backdoor criterion if it has no descendant of treatment and blocks every path into treatment; any valid set gives the same effect, and when the confounder is unmeasured the front-door criterion chains two identifiable effects through a mediator that carries everything.
- [[Bandit Regret Lower Bounds]] — Regret lower bounds for k-armed and linear bandits: the Bretagnolle-Huber inequality and divergence decomposition give sqrt((k-1)n)/27 minimax, an instance-dependent log n rate for consistent policies, high-probability limits, d*sqrt(n) for linear bandits and a case where optimism is arbitrarily suboptimal.
- [[Basel IRB Capital Vocabulary]] — The terms linking a default-probability model to bank capital (PD, LGD, EAD, expected vs unexpected loss, RWA) and the one-factor logic that makes capital grow far more slowly than expected loss as PD rises.
- [[Bayes Action and Loss Functions]] — A posterior is not a decision: write down what each wrong answer costs, average that loss over posterior draws and pick the minimiser; squared loss gives the mean, absolute loss the median, zero-one the mode, and asymmetric losses give answers no point estimate would.
- [[Bayes Factors and Composite Hypotheses]] — The odds form of Bayes's theorem multiplies prior odds by a likelihood ratio (the Bayes factor); a composite hypothesis gets the prior-weighted average of its sub-hypotheses' likelihoods, so how the alternative is specified decides the verdict.
- [[Bayes Rule and the Base Rate Fallacy]] — P(A|B) and P(B|A) share a numerator but not a denominator; Bayes' rule converts between them through the base rate, which is why a 99 percent accurate test of a rare event is mostly false positives and why a significant p-value is often a dud.
- [[Bayesian A/B Testing with Expected Loss]] — Draw from each arm's Beta posterior to get P(B > A), the lift interval and expected loss; ship when expected loss is below the smallest difference you care about, and report the lift size, since P(B > A) alone says nothing about it.
- [[Bayesian Measurement Error and Imputation]] — McElreath gives each uncertain or missing value its own parameter inside the model, with the observed value or the other predictors as the prior, instead of averaging the data or dropping incomplete cases.
- [[BCa Bootstrap Confidence Intervals]] — Percentile, BC and BCa intervals turn bootstrap replications into confidence intervals; BCa adds a bias value z0 and an acceleration a to reach second-order accuracy, at the cost of about 2000 replications and an extra jackknife step.
- [[Best-Arm Identification, Track-and-Stop and Sequential Halving]] — When exploring is free and only the final recommendation counts, cumulative-regret policies are the wrong tool: fixed-confidence identification has an instance lower bound that track-and-stop matches, and fixed-budget identification uses sequential halving.
- [[Beta-Binomial Updating and Credible Intervals]] — The Beta prior is conjugate to a Binomial rate, so Bayesian updating is addition of counts; the posterior gives a credible interval that says what people wish a confidence interval said, and separates MLE, MAP and posterior mean.
- [[BG/NBD Customer Lifetime Value]] — For non-contractual customers, BG/NBD needs only frequency, recency and observation time to estimate the chance a customer is still active and expected future orders; Gamma-Gamma adds order value, and LTV needs an explicit horizon and discount.
- [[Bootstrap Resampling and Its Failures]] — The bootstrap resamples the independent unit with replacement to get a standard error or interval without a formula, but it fails for extremes, tails that the sample never saw, and dependent rows resampled one at a time.
- [[Campaign Breakeven and Uplift Holdout]] — A contact campaign pays only for customers it persuades, not those who would buy anyway; compute the required lift as cost per contact over margin per extra order, then measure it against a randomised holdout, never against the contacted group's own past.
- [[Causal Design Selection Beyond Randomisation]] — When you cannot randomise, choose a design by how assignment happened (rollout with a pre-period, threshold, randomised nudge, rich covariates, one treated unit), name its untestable assumption, and check difference-in-differences pre-trends, since diverging trends almost doubled an estimate.
- [[CAVI and Stochastic Variational Inference]] — Mean-field variational inference gives each latent variable its own factor and updates one factor at a time (CAVI); stochastic variational inference replaces the full-data sweep with minibatch natural-gradient steps so the same model scales to millions of documents.
- [[Chart Colour Scales and Accessibility]] — Colour has three jobs (categorical, sequential, diverging) with one rule each; pin mappings by name, centre diverging scales on a real zero, and meet WCAG contrast, non-colour and table requirements.
- [[Chart Declutter and Direct Labelling]] — Strip every element that adds reader effort without understanding, set the style once, label data directly, and make the title state the finding; small multiples need a shared scale and grey context.
- [[Choosing a Chart from the Question]] — Pick the chart from the question being asked, and keep marks honest: bars start at zero, one y axis, several bin counts, uncertainty bands read as uncertainty, and titles that state the finding.
- [[CLT on Heavy-Tailed Metrics]] — The law of large numbers and central limit theorem only earn their keep at large N on zero-inflated heavy-tailed data: a nominal 95 percent normal interval on spend covered the truth 56 percent of the time at n=30 and needed tens of thousands of users to reach 95.
- [[Clustered Standard Errors]] — When rows share a group that was randomised or shares shocks, cluster the standard errors at that level: ignoring it understated the SE by half and inflated the false-positive rate to 28 percent, and it still fails with few clusters.
- [[Clustering Categorical and Mixed Data]] — One-hot plus k-means goes deaf to categories because dummy variance is small; FAMD and MCA recode before PCA, reducing first can hide groups, and the best method depends on which columns hold the groups.
- [[Clustering Control World Test]] — Before trusting behavioural clusters, run the pipeline on a control world drawn from one Gaussian: HDBSCAN's silhouette gate can pass it, UMAP-map silhouettes cannot tell structure from noise, and clusters must beat a one-line rule on a real decision.
- [[Cohort Retention Triangle]] — Cohorts hold customer age fixed so retention changes can be read as a cohort effect (who arrived) or a period effect (something hit everyone), with incomplete ages masked and channel value judged over 24 months, not at the first order.
- [[Combinatorial Bandits and Follow-the-Perturbed-Leader]] — A combinatorial bandit picks a subset of d components; with semi-bandit feedback mirror descent or follow-the-perturbed-leader with a geometric-resampling estimator gets regret of order sqrt(m d n) using one linear-optimisation oracle call per round.
- [[Confidence Interval Coverage and Wilson]] — A 95 percent interval is a claim about the procedure's long-run coverage, and the textbook Wald interval for a proportion fails badly for rare events at modest n where the Wilson interval holds up.
- [[Confounders, Mediators and Colliders]] — Control for a confounder, leave out a mediator if you want the total effect, and never condition on a collider; each mistake has a measured cost, and plotting the data beats trusting a single correlation coefficient.
- [[Cost-Based Decisions from a Posterior]] — Turn posterior draws and explicit costs into decisions: the newsvendor quantile short/(short+over) for stock, a cost-ratio probability threshold for blocking, and expected net value with regret risk across plausible costs for a ship decision.
- [[Counter-Metrics, Guardrails and Goodhart Variants]] — A guardrail is a global must-not-break; a counter-metric is paired with one target to expose hitting it badly, and it must come from a later funnel step; four Goodhart variants each need a different defence.
- [[Covariance Penalty Estimates of Prediction Error]] — Apparent error understates true prediction error by twice the covariance between each fit and its observation; Cp, AIC, SURE and a bootstrap penalty estimate that term from a model, while cross-validation estimates the average error of the algorithm without one.
- [[Covariate Balance and Overlap Checks]] — Before reading any outcome, check that treated and comparison groups are comparable using standardised mean differences, variance ratios and propensity overlap; fix the design on balance alone, and report the effective sample size since 5,710 weighted controls were worth 107.
- [[CUPED Variance Reduction]] — CUPED subtracts theta times the centred pre-experiment value of the same metric from each outcome, cutting variance by rho squared without bias because the covariate predates assignment; it equals regression adjustment and fails for new users or weak predictors.
- [[Dashboard API Exposure and Payload Cost]] — A list endpoint returning the whole table is insecure and slow; return aggregates by default, filter fields with a response model, cap and keyset-paginate row-level routes, and enforce limits server-side.
- [[Dashboard Design: Paired Metrics and Context]] — A dashboard serves a recurring decision with few contextualised metrics, each growth metric paired with a health metric; Streamlit reruns the script per interaction, and BI tools suit shared editing.
- [[Dashboard Stack Layers and Choosing a Route]] — Every dashboard has analysis, application and presentation layers; chart libraries are presentation only, and the route (BI tool, Python app, full stack) is chosen by audience, data and maintainer.
- [[Data Defect Correlation]] — Error of a sample mean equals the correlation between being recorded and the value, times sqrt((1-f)/f), times the standard deviation, so a large self-selected sample can be worth a few hundred random rows while reporting a tiny standard error.
- [[Data Generating Process Simulation Check]] — Before trusting an analysis, simulate data from a known process, run the estimator, and report bias, spread and coverage; a placebo run with a true zero effect exposes a model that invents effects.
- [[Data Science Curriculum Layers]] — The tree-shaped ML curriculum — statistical foundations, then supervised learning branching to model evaluation and independently to unsupervised/ensembles/Bayesian — plus the six-layer analytics progression that precedes it and the branching decision that ends it.
- [[Datetime Parsing, Frequency Aliases and Time Zones]] — Parse dates with an explicit format, use the explicit pandas 3 frequency aliases, and store timestamps in UTC so ambiguous days, renamed aliases and daylight-saving hours do not corrupt results.
- [[Delta Method for Ratio Metrics]] — A ratio of two per-user totals, such as revenue per session, needs a standard error that accounts for numerator and denominator co-varying; computing it as if sessions were the independent units understates it by about 40 percent.
- [[DetectGPT]] — DetectGPT flags text sampled from a model by comparing its log probability with that of T5-perturbed rewrites; the normalised gap is larger for model samples, giving zero-shot detection with no training data or watermark (self-reported).
- [[Dichotomising Continuous Variables]] — Splitting a continuous measurement into two groups, at the median or at the cutoff with the best p value, throws away information and inflates false positives; use regression on the continuous variable or a cutoff fixed in advance.
- [[Difference in Significance and Error Bar Overlap]] — Two bad shortcuts for comparing groups: calling one result significant and another not, and judging by whether error bars overlap. Compare the groups directly with a test or an interval for their difference.
- [[Difference-in-Differences as Two-Way Fixed Effects]] — A difference-in-differences estimate equals the treated-by-post coefficient in a two-way fixed effects regression, and rests on untestable parallel trends that pre-period event-study checks detect poorly.
- [[Distribution Selection for Metrics]] — Name the distribution behind a metric and you can usually name the test: Normal for measurements, Binomial for conversions, Poisson for counts, Exponential for gaps, lognormal for money, Beta for an uncertain rate.
- [[Divergences and Non-Centred Parameterisation]] — Hierarchical models create a funnel when the group scale is small, so NUTS diverges and underestimates the scale; sampling a standard normal and scaling it (non-centred form) changes the coordinates, not the model, and removes most divergences.
- [[Double Dipping and Selection on the Test Data]] — Using the same data to choose what to test and to test it, picking subjects for extreme values, or stopping a trial when the p value dips, biases results toward false positives and exaggerated effects; split the data or fix the plan in advance.
- [[EDA Audit Loop]] — What to do with a new extract before any test or model: fix the row unit, hunt impossible and missing values, pick summaries the shape allows, and write the findings and open questions down.
- [[Empirical Bayes: Tweedie's Formula and g-Modeling]] — With many parallel observations the prior can be learned from the data: Tweedie's formula corrects selection bias from the marginal density alone (f-modeling), while g-modeling fits the prior itself as an exponential family on a grid and gets standard errors by delta method or parametric bootstrap.
- [[Empirical CDFs for Comparing and Checking Distributions]] — A cumulative distribution function compares groups without the noise of a PMF, converts values to percentile ranks, generates random draws by inversion, and turns exponential, Pareto and lognormal fit checks into straight-line tests.
- [[Entropy Balancing]] — Entropy balancing finds control weights closest to uniform whose covariate moments equal the treated group's exactly, skipping the propensity model; limited overlap shows up as extreme weights, so check effective sample size alongside balance.
- [[Exp3 Adversarial Bandit Algorithms]] — Exp3 samples arms from exponential weights over importance-weighted reward estimates, giving sqrt(K T log K) regret against any reward sequence; Exp3-IX and Exp3.P add bias or confidence terms so the bound holds with high probability, not only in expectation.
- [[Exp3 for Adversarial Linear Bandits]] — Exponential weights over a finite action set, with least-squares loss estimates and an exploration mixture, gets regret that grows with dimension d and log of the action count instead of the action count; the same chapter group relates this adversarial model to stochastic linear bandits.
- [[Exp4 and Oracle-Based Contextual Bandits]] — Contextual regret competes with the best context-to-action policy in hindsight; Exp4 gets sqrt(2nk log M) over M experts but needs memory per expert, and a coordinate-descent method calls a classification oracle sublinearly often while keeping near-optimal regret.
- [[Expectation and Variance Rules]] — Expectations always add, even for dependent variables, while variances add only for uncorrelated ones; indicator variables turn counts into sums, and the law of total variance explains why knowing a group removes noise.
- [[Expectation-Maximisation for Gaussian Mixtures]] — EM fits a mixture by alternating soft cluster assignments (E-step) with weighted parameter refits (M-step); the likelihood never falls but only reaches a local peak, so restart several times and keep the best, and remember it gives a point estimate with no parameter uncertainty.
- [[Experiment Ramps and Overlapping Tests]] — A platform assigns users with a salted cryptographic hash per experiment layer so many tests overlap independently, ramps traffic from tiny shares because a catastrophe needs far less data than a planned effect, and audits the portfolio because winners' summed lifts overstate what shipped.
- [[Explain vs Predict Modelling]] — Explanation wants low bias on a coefficient and prediction wants low total error, so a wrong simple model can win held-out error at small n while the true form wins at large n; say which goal comes first.
- [[Explanatory Data Story and the Big Idea]] — Fix audience, ask and one-sentence point of view before opening a slide tool, size the decision with named assumptions and a break-even, and test the story with horizontal logic.
- [[Explore-Then-Commit Bandit Policy]] — Explore-then-commit plays every arm m times, then commits to the best empirical mean; its regret is m times the gaps plus a commitment-error term, and the best m depends on the unknown gap, which is the problem UCB removes.
- [[Exponential Families and Tilting]] — An exponential family reweights a base density by exp(alpha times a sufficient statistic); its maximum likelihood estimate solves a moment-matching equation, and it is the structure beneath Poisson, binomial, multinomial and generalised linear models.
- [[External Validity and Transportability]] — Randomisation buys internal validity only: whether an effect carries to another population depends on how the sample was selected, and reweighting fixes selection on measured variables but never on an unmeasured one.
- [[False-Discovery Rate, the Two-Groups Model and Local fdr]] — Benjamini-Hochberg control of the false-discovery rate has an empirical Bayes reading in the two-groups model, where the local fdr gives each case's probability of being null and an empirical null replaces N(0,1) when the z-histogram centre is too wide.
- [[Follow-the-Regularised-Leader and Mirror Descent]] — Two potential-based update rules for online linear optimisation: choosing the potential to fit the action set's geometry recovers exponential weights on the simplex and gives an efficient sqrt(d)-better adversarial linear bandit algorithm on the unit ball.
- [[Forecast Baselines and Rolling-Origin Backtest]] — A forecasting model earns trust only by beating free baselines (seasonal naive above all) across many rolling origins, scored with a scaled error plus bias, with the final origin held back as an untouched exam.
- [[Functional Form Misspecification]] — A linear control for a hump-shaped confounder biases the treatment effect threefold with zero coverage, flexible forms fix it, and a quadratic whose peak looks significant can be an artifact of a curve that never turns.
- [[Funnel Step Diagnosis]] — A flat end-to-end conversion can hide a broken step behind an unrelated gain; split into conditional step rates by device, use an unaffected segment as a difference-in-differences control, rule out tracking bugs, then price the loss and size the test the step's own traffic allows.
- [[G-Optimal Design and Phased Elimination]] — A G-optimal design picks a few arms to pull in proportion to a distribution that makes least-squares confidence widths uniformly small; phased elimination uses it each phase to get sqrt(d n log(nk)) regret on a fixed finite action set.
- [[Gaussian Processes and Bayesian Optimisation]] — A Gaussian process is a prior over functions that gives a prediction with an honest error bar; Bayesian optimisation uses it as a cheap surrogate and an acquisition function to pick the next expensive evaluation, but beats random search only when each fit is costly.
- [[Generalised Linear Models and Deviance]] — A GLM pairs a distribution with a link function and is fitted by maximum likelihood; deviance replaces the sum of squares for comparing models, while Pearson dispersion is the better check of the variance assumption.
- [[Generative Story Models]] — Build a Bayesian model by writing how the data could have been generated, top down, and translating each line into a distribution; a known measurement process (a changepoint, randomised response, serial numbers) goes into the model, not into a correction afterwards.
- [[Gittins Index and Bayesian Optimal Bandits]] — The Bayesian optimal bandit policy minimises regret averaged over a prior: it is computable by backwards induction for one arm, and for discounted problems with independent arms it is the Gittins index rule, though it is brittle when the prior is wrong.
- [[GLTR]] — GLTR scores each token's probability, rank and predictive entropy under a detection language model and colours the text by rank bucket; in a 35-student study the overlay raised human detection of fake text from 54% to 72% (self-reported).
- [[Good-Toulmin Missing-Species Estimator]] — The number of species or words not yet seen in a first sample can be projected from the counts of those seen once, twice and so on; the series is usable up to a doubling of the sample and a gamma prior extends it with less certainty.
- [[Grammar of Graphics]] — A chart is a composition of independent decisions (data, mark, encoding, statistic, scale, coordinates, facet), so chart choice becomes "which encoding makes my comparison easiest to read".
- [[Green-List LLM Watermark]] — Kirchenbauer et al.'s watermark adds a bias to a pseudo-random green subset of tokens at each sampling step and detects it with a z-test on the green-token count, without model access; on OPT-1.3B it flagged 98.4% of generations at z=4 (self-reported).
- [[Grid and Quadratic Approximation and Posterior Samples]] — McElreath's first-edition chapters compute a posterior on a grid or as a Gaussian at its peak, then answer every question by counting posterior samples: intervals, point estimates under a loss, predictions that carry parameter uncertainty.
- [[Grid Posteriors Over Several Parameters]] — Enumerate hypotheses on a grid and run one update routine that only needs a likelihood; with two parameters read joint, marginal and conditional posteriors, and with two levels solve each lower level first and feed its normalising constant up.
- [[Groupby Silent Group Loss and Pivot Defaults]] — Groupby drops NaN keys, hides empty categories or averages by default without a warning, so compare the sum of groups with the overall total and state every default.
- [[Groupby Split-Apply-Combine and Result Shapes]] — groupby splits a table, applies a function per group and recombines; agg, transform, filter and apply differ by the shape they return, and picking one starts from what a row of output should mean.
- [[Incrementality Measurement Ladder]] — Rank marketing evidence by what it can claim: attribution shares credit, a geo test measures the average return over a tested spend change, and a media mix model with adstock and saturation estimates marginal returns, which budgets move on.
- [[Independence Pitfalls]] — Pairwise independence does not give mutual independence, and two correlated events can be independent given a third; checking variables two at a time cannot prove there is no joint structure, and a shared cause creates correlation that vanishes when conditioned on.
- [[Inspection Paradox in Metrics]] — A metric sampled one way and quoted another differs: sampling by member weights each group by its size, so per-session averages exceed per-user averages and the two can move in opposite directions.
- [[Instrumental Variables and LATE]] — An instrument moves treatment but affects the outcome only through it, so the Wald ratio of the instrument's effect on outcome over its effect on treatment recovers the effect for compliers despite unmeasured confounding; weak first stages and an untestable exclusion restriction are the failure modes.
- [[Interactive Charts and When They Pay]] — Interaction moves work from author to reader; use it for exploration and monitoring, state the finding yourself for decisions, and know the shipping costs of plotly and Altair.
- [[Interference and Cluster-Randomised Experiments]] — When one user's arm changes another's outcome, as with fixed stock in a marketplace, a user-level A/B test overstates the effect (+30% measured against +19% real), so randomise by cluster, switchback or item and pay the design effect 1+(m-1)rho in variance.
- [[Jackknife Standard Error]] — The jackknife estimates a statistic's standard error from its n leave-one-out values; it matches classic formulas for smooth statistics but runs high and fails for non-smooth ones such as the median or a lowess curve.
- [[James-Stein Estimator and Shrinkage]] — With four or more normal means estimated at once, pulling each estimate toward the grand mean beats the separate maximum likelihood estimates on total squared error, at the cost of some individual cases getting worse.
- [[JavaScript Charting Ladder]] — D3 (elements and geometry, data join), Vega-Lite (JSON grammar compiled to Vega) and Observable Plot (marks and options) trade control against code volume; default to the highest rung that works.
- [[Label Horizon vs Sampling Window]] — A supervised frame's label horizon (the window over which the label's outcome was observed and finalised) is distinct from its label definition and from its sampling window; a public fraud set's two-day `Time` column is routinely misread as a horizon when the labeling window is unstated and unrecoverable — under an 'inferred horizon = unavailable' standard, such a frame fails, and applying that standard to one run but not its sibling is itself the finding.
- [[Laplace Mechanism and Sensitivity-Calibrated Noise]] — Dwork, McSherry, Nissim and coauthors prove that Laplace noise with scale equal to a query's L1 sensitivity over epsilon keeps answers epsilon-indistinguishable, even for adaptive query sequences, and that non-interactive mechanisms need exponentially large databases.
- [[Linear Interactions and Centring]] — McElreath makes a slope its own linear model to let an association depend on another variable, centres continuous predictors so main effects keep their meaning, and plots posterior predictions in place of reading a table of means.
- [[LinUCB and Linear Bandit Confidence Ellipsoids]] — LinUCB serves the arm whose upper confidence bound from a ridge regression is highest, with an ellipsoid confidence set that gives d*sqrt(n) regret for linear rewards; a news-recommendation deployment, a per-coordinate trick for sparse parameters and a self-normalised bound for adaptive actions complete the picture.
- [[Locomotive Problem: Estimators and Losses]] — Guessing how many locomotives a railway owns from one observed serial number gives different best answers under different criteria: the maximum likelihood, minimum squared error and unbiased estimators are i, 3i/2 and 2i, and a Bayesian posterior shows how weak one observation is.
- [[Log-Rank Test and Cox Partial Likelihood]] — Censored survival is summarised by life tables and Kaplan-Meier curves, compared across arms by summing observed-minus-expected deaths over periods, and regressed on covariates with a partial likelihood that leaves the baseline hazard unspecified.
- [[Logistic Regression Odds Ratios vs Marginal Effects]] — A logistic coefficient is a change in log-odds; exponentiating gives an odds ratio, but the change in probability depends on the starting rate, so report average marginal effects for business impact.
- [[Logistic Regression Separation]] — When a feature perfectly predicts the outcome, maximum likelihood diverges, giving a huge coefficient and an absurd standard error; a penalty or weakly informative prior stabilises it.
- [[Markov Chain Customer Value]] — A monthly state-transition table gives customer lifetime value in one linear solve, but a single pooled chain averages unlike customers and undervalues them (about 19% low in simulation); check the chain on a holdout and use a mixture of chains.
- [[Masked Relationships and Collinear Predictors]] — In McElreath's multiple-regression chapter, two predictors can hide each other's association with the outcome, two near-duplicate predictors split one effect, and a post-treatment predictor erases a real one; each is shown on a simulation or the primate-milk data.
- [[Matching and Weighting Estimators]] — Exact matching, coarsened exact matching, propensity matching, propensity weighting, entropy balancing and regression adjustment answer different questions (ATE, ATT, local ATT) and all landed 3 to 4 pounds above the truth because of an unlogged confounder; report who was kept and judge designs on balance before outcomes.
- [[Matplotlib Object Model and Seaborn Function Levels]] — Matplotlib is a tree of Figure, Axes and artists with two APIs (use the object-oriented one), and pandas and seaborn draw through it; seaborn splits into figure-level and axes-level functions; plus pandas shortcuts, library-version changes, format and pitfall notes.
- [[Maximum Entropy Likelihoods and Link Functions]] — McElreath's rule for choosing a likelihood is to take the maximum-entropy distribution that fits the outcome's constraints and attach a linear model through a link; the cost is that no coefficient gives a constant change on the outcome scale.
- [[Maximum Likelihood and Estimator Bias]] — An estimator is a recipe judged by bias, variance and MSE; maximum likelihood picks the parameter that makes the data most probable, gives standard errors from the curvature of the log-likelihood, and is a little biased at small n.
- [[MC Dropout]] — Keep dropout switched on at test time and average several stochastic forward passes; the paper argues this approximates Bayesian inference in a deep Gaussian process and gives the spread of the passes as a model-uncertainty signal at no change to training.
- [[MCMC and Metropolis Sampling]] — MCMC returns correlated samples from the posterior using only density ratios; step size decides whether a Metropolis chain works, effective sample size and R-hat tell whether it did, and gradient-based NUTS removes the tuning for continuous models.
- [[Metric Tree Decomposition]] — A metric tree splits a top number into identity edges that can be asserted and attributed exactly (log-mean weights) and dashed causal edges that need a test before anyone acts; a count north star needs a paired check.
- [[Minimum Detectable Effect and Sample Size]] — Sample size per arm is about 16 sigma squared over delta squared at 5 percent significance and 80 percent power, so halving the detectable effect quadruples the users and small relative lifts on rare outcomes need millions.
- [[Misleading Axes: Dual Axes and Truncated Bars]] — Two y scales let the author author the story, and a bar from a non-zero baseline exaggerates differences; use aligned panels, an index, and zero-based bars.
- [[Mixture Models and Label Switching]] — A two-cluster mixture can be fitted with sampled discrete labels or, far faster, by summing the labels out of the likelihood; symmetric components then swap identity across chains, which an ordering constraint or informative priors fixes.
- [[Model Selection Criteria]] — R-squared never rejects extra terms, adjusted R-squared, AIC, BIC, likelihood-ratio tests and cross-validation each trade fit against complexity differently: BIC found a true quadratic 98 percent of the time at n=800, AIC 77 percent.
- [[Multilevel vs Fixed Effects vs Clustered Standard Errors]] — Pick by question: clustered SEs when groups are a nuisance, fixed effects (or a within-between model) when the group effect may correlate with the predictor, multilevel when group-level estimates are the question; plain random intercepts can be biased where the others are not.
- [[Multiple Comparisons Corrections]] — Running many tests makes a false positive likely, 64 percent for 20 tests at 5 percent, so adjust: Bonferroni and Holm control the chance of any false positive, Benjamini-Hochberg controls the share of false discoveries and keeps more power.
- [[NeuralLinear Thompson Sampling]] — NeuralLinear does Bayesian linear regression on a network's last hidden layer and samples from it for Thompson sampling; in a 2018 benchmark of about 15 approximate-posterior methods on contextual bandits it was robust and easy to tune, while methods that tie representation to uncertainty suffered online.
- [[Newsvendor Critical Ratio]] — For a one-off order under uncertain demand, order the quantile of demand equal to underage cost over underage plus overage cost, not the mean; the gain is real but small and forecast error matters more than the rule.
- [[No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS)]] — NUTS extends Hamiltonian Monte Carlo so the number of leapfrog steps is chosen per iteration by doubling a trajectory until it turns back, and dual averaging tunes the step size during burn-in.
- [[Non-Stationary Bandits and Tracking Regret]] — When the best arm can change, regret is measured against a sequence with at most m-1 changes; plain Exp3 fails, clipping the simplex fixes it with an efficient mirror-descent policy, and no stochastic policy can match the stationary log-regret rate under abrupt changes.
- [[Observed Fisher Information and Ancillary Conditioning]] — The maximum likelihood estimate's accuracy can be stated from the curvature of the observed log-likelihood, and conditioning on an ancillary statistic such as the sample size gives the inference that matches the data actually seen.
- [[OEC, Guardrails and North Star Metrics]] — Experiment metrics split into data-quality checks, one pre-agreed overall evaluation criterion, guardrails tested for non-inferiority against a margin, and diagnostics; a correct test on the wrong metric gives a confident wrong answer, and not significant is not a guardrail pass.
- [[OLS Influence and Collinearity Diagnostics]] — One high-leverage point can flip a regression slope, flagged by leverage and Cook's distance, while near-duplicate regressors inflate standard errors without hurting prediction, flagged by the variance inflation factor.
- [[Open POI Data Sources]] — Measured comparison of open point-of-interest sources for a city venue catalogue — OSM tags, Wikidata, Wikivoyage listings, Foursquare OS Places and Overture Maps — covering licence, channel, per-field fill rate, freshness and bias, with the conclusion that open POI data is a triage and proposal signal behind a human gate, never a write path.
- [[Optimal Matching and Journey Sequences]] — Optimal matching measures how alike two customer journeys are as the cheapest edits turning one state string into another; clustering that distance describes journey shapes after the fact but cannot value or predict, and costs are a modelling choice.
- [[Ordered Logit and the Cumulative Link]] — An ordered categorical outcome gets K-1 cutpoints on the log-cumulative-odds scale, and a linear predictor phi is subtracted from every cutpoint so that a positive coefficient raises the expected response.
- [[P-Value Misreadings]] — A p-value is the chance of data at least this extreme if the null were true, not the chance the null is true, not an effect size, and not evidence of no effect when large; with millions of users a negligible lift is highly significant.
- [[Pandas Copy-on-Write and Chained Assignment]] — Since pandas 3.0 every indexing or method result behaves as a copy, so chained assignment does nothing and one .loc call is the only way to update part of a frame.
- [[Pandas Index Design, MultiIndex and Reindex]] — Making a real key the index turns lookups into plain label access, but non-unique indexes change the return type and MultiIndex slicing needs sorting; reindex fixes the row list of a report.
- [[Pandas Label Alignment and Dtypes]] — pandas matches values by label rather than position and ties behaviour to per-column dtypes, so NaN promotion and text-typed numbers are the first things to check on any frame.
- [[Pandas loc, iloc and the Integer Index Trap]] — loc selects by label and iloc by position; they agree only until a filter or sort leaves gaps in the default integer labels, after which the same-looking code returns different rows.
- [[Pandas Merge Validation and Many-to-Many Blow-up]] — A pandas merge can silently drop or multiply rows; state how, set validate, then compare row counts and one total before and after every join.
- [[Pandas Missing Data Decisions]] — Pandas can count, fill and drop gaps but cannot say what a gap means; the treatment follows from why the value is missing.
- [[Panel Data Fixed Effects]] — Fixed effects remove all stable differences between units by using only within-unit variation, turning a pooled estimate of -1.0 into the true 0.5, but they absorb time-invariant variables and do not remove time-varying confounders.
- [[Partial Monitoring Games]] — In partial monitoring the learner sees a signal instead of its loss; finite adversarial games fall into exactly four regret classes (0, sqrt n, n^(2/3), linear), decided by whether neighbouring actions are locally or globally observable.
- [[Partial Pooling and Multilevel Models]] — A multilevel model estimates every group together so each borrows strength in proportion to how little data it has (weight = tau^2 / (tau^2 + sigma^2/n)); it beats both no pooling and complete pooling in simulation, but boundary estimates and few groups make the variance parts fragile.
- [[Partial Pooling for Segment Winners]] — When an experiment reports a lift per segment, the raw winner is overstated; pooling segment estimates removed most of the exaggeration in simulation (+2.93 to +0.09) but picked the best segment only modestly better, and it fails for outliers, wrong centres and when the prior on tau is wrong.
- [[Pearson, Spearman Correlation and R-Squared]] — Pearson correlation is the mean product of standard scores and sees only straight-line association, Spearman applies it to ranks, and R-squared is the squared correlation read as the cut in squared prediction error; none of them shows causation.
- [[Peeking and Sequential Testing]] — Checking a fixed-horizon p-value repeatedly inflates false positives to 25% at 20 looks, so if people will look use group-sequential boundaries (O'Brien-Fleming, Pocock) or always-valid mSPRT p-values, which pay for looks with a stricter threshold.
- [[Peeking Under Bayesian Stopping Rules]] — Bayesian tests are not immune to peeking: a daily P(B > A) > 0.95 rule shipped 23.8% of no-effect changes in simulation, and an expected-loss rule ships quickly but needs a minimum sample size to stop harmful launches.
- [[Per-User Metrics Depend on Window Length]] — Any metric with the user as its unit grows with the observation window because users get more chances to return, so comparing periods of unequal length manufactures a change; match windows or use sessions.
- [[Permutation Test]] — A permutation test builds the null distribution by shuffling group labels, needing few assumptions; classical t-tests are approximations to it and agree closely on well-behaved data.
- [[Poisson Offsets and Overdispersion]] — Model rates by adding log exposure as an offset in a log-link count model, then check dispersion: when variance exceeds the mean the Poisson standard error is too small by a factor of three and robust or negative binomial errors are needed.
- [[Population Stability Index]] — PSI compares a score's current distribution with the one a model was built on, bin by bin, needing no outcomes, so it warns of drift years before defaults arrive; its 0.10 and 0.25 thresholds are conventions.
- [[Post-Treatment Segments and Simpson's Paradox]] — A trend can reverse inside every subgroup of aggregated data; which table to trust depends on the causal story, so segment on traits fixed before treatment, never on something the treatment changes, and in experiments keep the allocation constant across periods.
- [[Posterior Predictive Checks]] — A precise posterior can still be wrong; simulate new datasets from the fitted model and compare them with the real data on a quantity the model was not tuned to match, ideally also reporting the whole probability band rather than a line.
- [[Potential Outcomes and Selection Bias]] — Each unit has two potential outcomes but only one is seen, so a naive treated-minus-untreated comparison equals the effect on the treated plus selection bias; opt-in comparisons mostly measure selection (1.21 against a true 0.30), and controlling for the selection driver recovers it only if nothing unmeasured matters.
- [[Precomputed Labels, Not Refits on Filter]] — Cluster ids are arbitrary and refits on filtered data move customers between groups, so a batch job should publish labels and the app should only read them.
- [[Predicting from a Posterior as a Mixture]] — A prediction should average the outcome distribution over the whole posterior of the rate, not plug in its best value; a Poisson goal model gives win, tie and series probabilities this way, and uncertainty in a rate matters when the response is non-linear.
- [[Prior Choice and Prior Predictive Checks]] — A wide prior is not uninformative once a model transforms it; simulate outcomes from the prior before seeing data, pick weakly informative priors on a standardised scale, and remember priors wash out with data but a zero-mass region never does.
- [[Prior Shift Correction for Oversampled Defaults]] — When bad outcomes are oversampled, a model's probabilities describe a population that does not exist; adding a constant to the log odds fixes the level without changing ranking, so Gini and KS cannot reveal the error, and the approve/decline cutoff must come from costs on the corrected probability.
- [[Pseudoreplication and Intraclass Correlation]] — Rows from the same unit are correlated, so m rows per unit with intraclass correlation rho inflate the variance of a mean by 1+(m-1)rho; analysing at a finer level than the randomisation unit makes standard errors too small and null effects look significant.
- [[Publication and Outcome Reporting Bias]] — Unshared data, unpublished studies and unreported outcomes skew the visible evidence toward extreme results; register protocols, share data and code, publish negative results and check study power against the share of positive results.
- [[Queue Staffing with Little's Law and Erlang C]] — Little's law (L = lambda W) validates a queue log exactly and gives waits from a head count; Erlang C describes steady state but a shift that starts empty runs below it; price the extra worker and solve rosters as a linear programme.
- [[Ranking by Lower Credible Bound]] — Sort items with few observations by a low percentile of each item's Beta posterior instead of the raw ratio, so 1 upvote with 0 downvotes cannot outrank 999 and 1; the same fix applies to star ratings and to LLM prompts compared on eval pass rate.
- [[Rate and Mix Decomposition]] — Split a change in any aggregate into a rate (within-segment) effect and a mix effect by holding one fixed at a time; the split depends on order, so report it roughly, and it decomposes a change without proving cause.
- [[Ratio Metrics and the Denominator Trap]] — When treatment can change a ratio's denominator, the ratio can fall while the numerator and the business improve; never report a ratio without its parts, choose ratio of averages or average of ratios deliberately, and take the standard error with the user as the independent unit.
- [[Ratio of Sums vs Mean of Rates]] — A group's rate is the sum of numerators over the sum of denominators; averaging per-row rates weights tiny and huge rows equally and can reverse the ranking of groups.
- [[Regression Covariate Adjustment]] — OLS on a treatment dummy reproduces the t-test, and adding pre-assignment covariates shrinks the standard error without biasing a randomised estimate, while adding post-treatment or omitting confounders in observational data changes the answer entirely.
- [[Regression Discontinuity]] — Units just either side of a cutoff are nearly comparable, so fitting a line on each side and reading the jump estimates the effect at the cutoff; report several bandwidths and check for pile-up at the cutoff, and do not extrapolate away from it.
- [[Regression Splines, Smoothing Splines and GAMs]] — Polynomials, step functions, regression splines, smoothing splines, local regression and additive models all fit a non-linear curve while staying a linear or additive model; each has one knob (degree, knots, lambda, span) that cross-validation or an F-test sets.
- [[Regularisation as a Prior]] — Ridge is the MAP estimate under a Normal prior and lasso the MAP under a Laplace prior, with penalty strength equal to prior precision; lasso's exact zeros come from the MAP's geometry, not from a Bayesian belief in sparsity.
- [[Rendering Many Points: WebGL, Datashader and Bundle Weight]] — Measured costs of drawing many points (SVG vs WebGL vs server-side datashader), shipping chart libraries, and mapping; ask first whether the points are needed at all.
- [[Rerunnable Cleaning Loader with Assertions]] — Keep the raw file untouched and put every cleaning step in one function that asserts what must be true and prints row counts, so the next export runs through the same checks.
- [[Researcher Degrees of Freedom and Prespecification]] — Choosing measures, covariates, exclusions and stopping points after seeing the data inflates false positives even without intent; plan the analysis first, register it, analyse blind where possible and replicate before publishing.
- [[Revenue Bridge Query]] — A revenue bridge labels each customer-month new, returning or reactivated with a lag window over a CTE chain, so a flat-looking total splits into parts that move in opposite directions; the labelling gap is a stated choice and the total must reconcile to source.
- [[RFM Segmentation Validated on a Holdout]] — RFM segments are predictions and must be scored on a period after the snapshot; doing so shows that frequent buyers who went silent are mostly gone (iso-value curves bend backwards), that clustering adds little over R and F, and that value is concentrated but only partly predictable.
- [[Robbins Empirical Bayes Formula]] — With many parallel Poisson cases, the posterior mean for one case can be read off the counts of how many cases showed x and x+1 events, with no prior assumed; a fitted parametric prior is steadier where counts are thin.
- [[Robust Standard Errors]] — When residual spread changes with the fitted value, classical OLS standard errors are wrong while the coefficients stay fine; HC3 heteroskedasticity-robust errors fix the standard error, not the estimate.
- [[Robust Summaries for Skewed Metrics]] — When mean and median disagree, the tail is running the summary: choose location, spread and correlation measures the shape allows, and report percentiles and the mean-to-median ratio instead of one number.
- [[Safety Stock, EOQ and Service Levels]] — EOQ balances order and holding cost on a flat cost curve; safety stock is z times the spread of lead-time demand including lead-time variance, and a flat reorder point misses its service target in seasons where demand runs higher.
- [[Sample Ratio Mismatch]] — Compare arm counts with the designed split using a chi-square test before reading any outcome; a failure means users were lost or added non-randomly, the test is stopped rather than reweighted, and A/A tests validate the whole pipeline.
- [[Selection-Aware Inference: FCR, Scheffe Intervals and Bagged Estimates]] — When the model or the reported cases are chosen from the same data, ordinary intervals are too short: simultaneous and false-coverage-rate intervals widen them, bootstrapping the whole select-then-fit algorithm gives honest standard errors, and bagging smooths a jumpy selector.
- [[Sensitivity Analysis for Unmeasured Confounding]] — E-values and Rosenbaum bounds ask what hidden confounder would erase an effect entirely and so can look reassuring while real bias is smaller; the whiteboard bias approximation gamma times delta (hidden effect per SD times remaining imbalance) sizes the bias, and refuters only catch pipeline errors.
- [[Shifted Beta Geometric Retention Model]] — The sBG gives each customer a constant per-period churn probability drawn from a beta distribution, so aggregate retention rises with age without anyone becoming loyal, and it extrapolates a cohort's survival far better than curve fits.
- [[Simulate the Joint and Slice for a Conditional]] — When the forward direction (size given age) is easy to simulate but the inverse (age given size) is not, simulate the joint distribution and slice it at the observed value, then test each assumption such as serial correlation by re-running.
- [[Small-N Trap and Shrinkage]] — Averages from small groups are noisy, so the best and worst groups of any league table are the smallest ones even when no group differs; show N beside every rate, rank by a lower bound, or shrink toward the overall rate with pseudo-observations.
- [[SQL Date Spine and Window Frames]] — Grouping drops periods with no rows, which inflates averages and lies on charts; start from a table of every period and left join. Also: explicit window frames, lag-by-rows pitfalls, QUALIFY for top-N and a one-scan funnel with FILTER.
- [[SQL Fan-Out and Source Reconciliation]] — Joining before aggregating repeats rows of the one side once per match and inflates sums with no error; aggregate the many side to the one side's grain first, and reconcile every report total to the source.
- [[SQL Sessionisation and Gaps-and-Islands]] — Two window tricks for grouping consecutive rows without loops: a running sum of gap flags numbers sessions, and a running index minus row_number is constant along an unbroken run so it serves as an island key.
- [[Staggered Rollout Bias in Two-Way Fixed Effects]] — With staggered adoption and effects that grow, one TWFE coefficient can be badly wrong because already-treated units serve as controls; a clean comparison against never-treated units recovers the effect.
- [[Standardised Survival and Pricing an Intervention]] — Turn a fitted hazard model into two survival curves (everyone treated, nobody treated) averaged over the real customer mix, so the gap becomes months gained and money; valid only if the observed covariates explain who got treated.
- [[Statistical Test Selection]] — Pick the test from the data type and design: Welch by default for two means, paired when rows pair, chi-square or Fisher for counts by expected cell size, rank tests for ordinal data but not for questions about totals.
- [[Stepwise Selection and Overfitting on Noise]] — Stepwise regression with more candidate variables than observations can fit pure noise almost perfectly, and its final model is biased and hard to read; check fit on separate data, or use the lasso or no selection.
- [[Stratified and Cluster Survey Design]] — Stratify to guarantee coverage of known groups, allocate proportionally unless one statistic dominates (Neyman allocation helps one number and hurts others), and cluster only when travel cost beats the design-effect loss.
- [[Sub-Gaussian Tail Bounds and Confidence Radii]] — A sigma-subgaussian variable has an exponentially small tail, which turns the sample mean into a finite-time confidence bound of radius sqrt(2 sigma^2 log(1/delta)/n); this is the bound a UCB index is built from, and the CLT cannot replace it.
- [[Survey Error Sources and Nonresponse Bias]] — A margin of error measures only sampling error from a probability sample; coverage, nonresponse and measurement errors are larger and do not shrink with more replies, and nonresponse bias comes from the link between answering and the answer, not the response rate.
- [[Survey Weighting, Raking and Effective Sample Size]] — Weighting respondents to known population totals (post-stratification or raking) removes only bias carried by the auxiliary variables; it inflates variance by Kish's 1 + L, and the margin must be multiplied by deft and still ignores leftover bias.
- [[Survival Analysis for Customer Lifetimes]] — Customer lifetimes are censored, so averaging them is biased; Kaplan-Meier, Cox hazard ratios, time-varying covariates, competing risks and a person-period logit each handle one trap, and a single hazard ratio can hide an effect that wears off.
- [[Synthetic Control Method]] — With one treated unit and many untreated donors, fit non-negative weights summing to one on the pre-period so the weighted donors track the treated unit, then read the post-launch gap; inference comes from placebo runs, not conventional standard errors.
- [[Thompson Sampling and Bandit Regret]] — Thompson sampling draws one sample from each arm's posterior and pulls the highest, so exploration is automatic and fades with evidence; regret measures the cost, and which rule wins depends on the gaps and horizon, so simulate your own.
- [[Time-Series Leakage: Shift and Rolling Windows]] — shift and rolling windows build comparisons and features from the past, but a negative shift or a centred window reads the future and leaks, and count-based windows misbehave on irregular rows.
- [[Time-Series Resampling, Bins and Gaps]] — Absent rows hide gaps in time data, and resample is a time-bin groupby whose closed and label edges, unequal period lengths and fill rules change the numbers.
- [[TopRank and Click Models for Ranking Bandits]] — Ranking l items into m slots from click feedback is learnable without treating each ranking as an arm: a click model ties clicks to item attractiveness and position, and TopRank compares items only within shared blocks.
- [[Unseen Species Hierarchical Dirichlet Model]] — Estimate how many species exist, how prevalent each is and how many new ones more sampling would find, with a Dirichlet prior over prevalences nested under a prior on the species count, simulated forward for predictions.
- [[Upper Confidence Bound Bandit Algorithms]] — UCB plays the arm with the highest empirical mean plus a confidence bonus, acting as if the world were as good as plausible; the confidence level sets the variant (horizon-based, anytime, minimax, KL), and each fixes a weakness of the one before.
- [[Variational Inference and the ELBO]] — Variational inference replaces an intractable posterior with a simple q chosen by maximising the ELBO (reconstruction minus KL to the prior), whose gap to the log evidence equals the KL to the true posterior; it scales well but mean-field versions understate variance under correlation.
- [[Varying Slopes and the LKJ Correlation Prior]] — Varying slopes give each cluster an intercept and a slope drawn from one multivariate Gaussian whose correlation matrix has an LKJcorr prior; a non-centred Cholesky form removes the divergences the centred form produces.
- [[Visual Encoding Accuracy Ranking]] — Readers judge some visual channels far more accurately than others, so put the comparison that matters on the most accurate channel and choose the chart from the question.
- [[WAIC, KL Divergence and Akaike Weights]] — McElreath builds the target of model comparison from entropy and K-L divergence, estimates out-of-sample deviance with AIC, DIC or WAIC, and uses Akaike weights to average predictions instead of picking one model.
- [[Weekly Seasonality Forecasting Methods]] — Weekly data has a non-integer yearly period (about 52.18 weeks) and moving holidays; STL+ETS, dynamic harmonic regression and Prophet handle it differently, and most of their edge comes from knowing the calendar.
- [[Weight-of-Evidence Logistic Scorecard]] — A credit scorecard bins each characteristic, converts bins to weight of evidence, fits a logistic regression, and prints the result as additive points; it matches gradient boosting within noise on small data while staying defensible line by line.
- [[Winner's Curse]] — When a test is underpowered, the estimates that reach significance are exaggerated: a true 1 percent lift with a 1 percent standard error had power 0.17, significant estimates averaged 2.5 times the truth, and some had the wrong sign.
- [[Writing a Metric Definition Two Analysts Agree On]] — A metric definition fixes population, finality, window, clock, unit, owner and version in a spec that code reads; immature cohorts read as falls (censoring) and the function should return None rather than zero.

## Data Science

- [[AdaBoost as Stagewise Exponential-Loss Fitting]] — AdaBoost reweights misclassified cases so the next tree faces a coin flip; statistically it is forward-stagewise fitting of exponential loss, whose population minimiser is half the logit, and a nonnegative lasso can prune the resulting tree ensemble.
- [[Adam, AdamW and Decoupled Weight Decay]] — L2 regularisation inside Adam is not weight decay (weight_decay 0.1 collapsed to chance while AdamW trained fine), and learning rate matters more than optimiser choice: a sweep by factors of ten moves accuracy from 0.174 to 0.961 on one model.
- [[AlgoPerf: Time-to-Result Benchmark for Training Algorithms]] — AlgoPerf scores training algorithms by wall-clock time to reach a fixed validation and test target on 8 workloads, with tuning counted inside the protocol; Dahl et al. report that its baselines rank NadamW with tuned beta1 first among the strict-budget runs.
- [[Auditing Selection Rates With Intervals and Intersections]] — Report impact ratios with counts and delta-method intervals, never as a pass mark; small groups can read anything, and margins can all clear 0.8 while a pre-chosen crossing fails.
- [[Autoencoder Bottleneck vs PCA and Anomaly Scores]] — An autoencoder beats PCA on reconstruction only at tight codes (2 to 8 dims) and loses at 32, a denoising variant trades clean accuracy for repair, and the best reconstruction is not the best anomaly detector: compute PCA first.
- [[Bag of Words, n-grams and TF-IDF]] — Sparse count and TF-IDF document vectors: how vocabulary controls, n-grams and idf weighting shape features, and why the vectoriser must be fitted inside the training fold.
- [[Batch Norm Eval Mode and Small-Batch Failure]] — Batch norm uses batch statistics in train mode and running statistics in eval mode, so forgetting model.eval() costs about 9 accuracy points at batch 32, tiny batches break it, and layer norm removes the batch dependence.
- [[BERTopic: Clustering Embeddings with Class-Based TF-IDF]] — BERTopic embeds documents, reduces them with UMAP, clusters with HDBSCAN and names each cluster with class-based TF-IDF; the paper reports it competitive with LDA, NMF, CTM and Top2Vec, with no variance or significance tests.
- [[Bias-Variance Decomposition and Model Complexity]] — Expected squared error splits into bias squared, variance and irreducible noise; complexity trades the first two, and the symptom table says which side to fix.
- [[BiDAF Bi-Directional Attention Flow]] — BiDAF links a question and a paragraph with memory-less attention in both directions and passes per-word vectors on to a modeling layer instead of summarising the paragraph early.
- [[Big Transfer (BiT) Generalist Pretraining]] — BiT scales supervised pre-training of ResNets with GroupNorm and Weight Standardization, then fine-tunes with a fixed size-based heuristic, BiT-HyperRule, instead of a per-task search.
- [[Blocking and Fellegi-Sunter Match Weights]] — Entity matching first cuts candidate pairs with cheap blocking keys, whose recall caps everything downstream, then scores each pair by summed log(m/u) evidence with a three-way link, review, reject decision.
- [[Bottleneck Adapters for Parameter-Efficient Transfer]] — Adapters are small trainable modules inserted into a frozen pretrained network: Rebuffi et al. (2017) added residual 1x1-convolution adapters to a ResNet for ten image domains, and Houlsby et al. (2019) added serial bottleneck adapters to BERT, both nearly matching full fine-tuning (all numbers self-reported).
- [[Build it Break it Fix it (Dialogue Safety)]] — Dinan et al. train an offensive-language classifier in rounds where crowdworkers write messages that fool the current model, then retrain on them; the adversarially trained model beat a standard-trained one on adversarial test rounds (self-reported).
- [[Calibrated Score, Deterministic Decision Rule]] — A ranked score is not a decision: in tabular ML the model supplies a probability and deterministic code turns it into an action with a cost-based threshold. That makes calibration a correctness requirement, puts the target definition (ability vs propensity vs uplift) ahead of model choice, and makes logistic regression the required explainability floor.
- [[Categorical Encoding Choices]] — One-hot, ordinal, frequency and target encoding suit different models and cardinalities; fit the encoder on training data and handle unseen categories.
- [[CCNet Web Crawl Corpus Pipeline]] — CCNet deduplicates Common Crawl paragraphs before language identification, then buckets each language's documents by perplexity under a Wikipedia language model.
- [[Chart Parsing, PCFGs and Structural Ambiguity]] — Why parsers need a chart and probabilities: attachment ambiguity multiplies parses with the Catalan numbers, backtracking recursive descent loops on left recursion, and a PCFG ranks parses by the product of production probabilities.
- [[Choosing a Metric From the Cost of Errors]] — A loss rewards a specific answer (mean, median, quantile), MAPE rewards forecasting low, balanced accuracy hides a cost ratio of N:P, and MCC cannot respond to costs; pick the metric from the decision first.
- [[Choosing an Encoder vs CNN vs LSTM]] — Decide by score at your data size, fit to the problem's structure, and cost at your length: a CNN won on small digits at every size, the encoder aced long-range tasks and lost local-pattern ones, and attention cost grows as n squared; take the learning curve on your own data.
- [[Choosing k and Naming Clusters Honestly]] — Scale and choose features before clustering, expect BIC, silhouette and seed agreement to disagree on k, shuffle columns as a null check, and only name clusters that survive reseeding.
- [[Choosing Which Rows to Label: Active Learning and Rules]] — Rules can stand in for many hand labels but learned rule weights lose to a plain vote at low label density; uncertainty sampling beat random on generated data and lost AUC on real data, and its labelled set is biased.
- [[Classification Metrics, Confusion Matrix and Thresholds]] — Read accuracy against a dummy baseline, derive precision, recall, F1, balanced accuracy and MCC from the confusion matrix, and move the threshold on validation data.
- [[Clustering Stability and Symmetry]] — In the large-sample limit, a centre-based or spectral clustering is stable when the objective has a unique best solution and unstable when a symmetry of the data swaps equally good ones, so stability under resampling does not tell you whether k is right.
- [[Clustering Validation Against Named Baselines]] — Customer clustering is only evidence of structure when its silhouette beats a random partition and the trivial k=2 split computed in the same call, when unsupervised leakage analogues (restated timestamp columns, scaler fit outside resamples, a sum beside its own decomposition) are ruled out by name, and when a value-reading matrix-health rule stops a degenerate matrix escaping through the 'structure is weak' clause.
- [[Code Completion Poisoning]] — Schuster et al. show that adding crafted files to a code autocompleter's training corpus, or fine-tuning on them, makes it rank an insecure completion first in attacker-chosen contexts, with targeted variants that fire only in chosen repos (self-reported).
- [[CodeRL Unit-Test Actor-Critic]] — CodeRL finetunes a pretrained code model (CodeT5-770M) with an actor-critic loop that uses unit-test outcomes as rewards, then at inference refines passing programs and repairs failing ones from error messages (Critic Sampling); it set new APPS pass@k results in 2022 (self-reported).
- [[Collocations and Pointwise Mutual Information]] — PMI scores word pairs by how much more often they co-occur than chance; it overrates rare pairs, so it needs a minimum count, while raw frequency surfaces only common pairs.
- [[Colorization as Classification over Quantised Colour Bins]] — Zhang, Isola and Efros predict a distribution over 313 quantised ab colour bins per pixel, reweight by colour rarity and decode with an annealed mean; the paper reports that 32% of AMT trials judged its output real, and tests colorization as a self-supervised pretext task (self-reported).
- [[Compacter Adapters]] — Compacter builds adapter weights from shared Kronecker factors and per-adapter rank-one factors, so a frozen T5-base trains 0.047 to 0.073% of its parameters yet matches full fine-tuning on GLUE and SuperGLUE (self-reported).
