- [[Compound Scaling of Depth, Width and Resolution (EfficientNet)]] — Compound scaling grows a ConvNet's depth, width and input resolution together with one coefficient instead of scaling a single dimension; EfficientNet applies it to a searched B0 baseline, and the paper reports 84.3% ImageNet top-1 for B7 with 66M parameters (self-reported).
- [[Contrastive Self-Supervised Pretraining]] — Learn embeddings without labels by pulling positives together and pushing negatives apart (triplet loss, SimCLR, MoCo) or by avoiding negatives with a predictor plus moving-average target (BYOL), or by masking and reconstructing patches; collapse is the central failure and toy-scale runs do not reproduce paper gains.
- [[Convolution, Shift Sensitivity and Parameter Sharing]] — A convolution reuses one small filter at every position (sharing plus sparse connections), so it survives shifts that wreck an MLP, but pooling and the final linear layer keep it from true invariance; augmentation buys invariance only for the shifts shown.
- [[Cost-Complexity Pruning of Decision Trees]] — ISLP grows a large tree and prunes it along a one-parameter family of subtrees indexed by alpha, picking alpha by k-fold CV; on its Boston example the pruned single tree has about twice the test MSE of a bagged forest.
- [[Counterfactual Evaluation with Inverse Propensity Scoring]] — Logs only record what the old system showed; inverse propensity scoring reweights logged events by one over the logging probability to estimate an unshipped policy's value, at the price of variance, so log propensities, keep some randomisation and report effective sample size.
- [[Cross-Validation Fold Spread and Choosing the Splitter]] — Fold spread sizes up noise but is not a clean standard error; compare models fold by fold, and pick the splitter (stratified, group, forward-in-time) that mimics how new data will arrive.
- [[Cross-Validation Variants and Nested Search]] — Pick the splitter that mirrors how the model meets new data, search hyperparameters on a log scale, and use nested CV because the best CV score is optimistic.
- [[D-Adaptation (Learning-Rate-Free Optimisation)]] — D-Adaptation sets the learning rate by tracking a running lower bound on the distance from the start point to a minimiser, with no line search or extra gradient evaluations.
- [[Data Sufficiency Refusal]] — A pure, never-raising guard reads the minimum positives in any CV fold against a stated floor and, when it fails, withholds the cost-sensitive metrics entirely — a run that refuses to answer is distinguishable from a run that failed and from a run that scored badly, and exits 0 because refusing was the correct output.
- [[Density Estimators: KDE Bandwidth, GMM Components and the Generative Classifier]] — Kernel density estimation and Gaussian mixtures model a data distribution rather than predict a label; choose bandwidth or component count by held-out likelihood or AIC/BIC, and fit one density per class to get a Bayesian generative classifier.
- [[Dilated Causal Convolutions]] — Convolutions with exponentially increasing dilation factors that preserve temporal causality while growing the receptive field exponentially with depth — the key architectural innovation in WaveNet for modeling long-range audio dependencies efficiently.
- [[DNC Memory Allocation and Temporal Links]] — The DNC extends the Neural Turing Machine with a usage vector, allocation weighting, a retention vector and a temporal link matrix, so a controller can free, reuse and re-read memory in order; a textbook TensorFlow reimplementation reaches 15.78% mean error on 20 bAbI tasks and fails badly on path finding.
- [[Drift Seen and Unseen in an Automated Router]] — With a frozen router, covariate drift trips a label-free alarm while the error stays fine, whereas prior and concept drift can leave the alarm green and double the error on automatically paid cases; recalibration fixes only part.
- [[Drift Types and What Monitors Can See]] — Covariate shift is loud and harmless, prior and concept shift are silent to input monitors, and feature skew can break a model while the score monitor barely moves; bin coded columns by level.
- [[Dropout and Input Distortion as Ridge Regression]] — For linear regression, dropout's expected score equations are a generalised ridge regression and iid noise on the inputs is approximately ordinary ridge, which is why heavily parametrised networks stay usable when regularised.
- [[Early and Late Dropout]] — Liu et al. switch dropout (or stochastic depth) on only for the first epochs to help underfitting vision models, and only after the first epochs for overfitting ones; the reported gains are small against seed noise.
- [[EASE and Item-Based Neighbourhood Recommenders]] — EASE is a linear item-item model with a zero diagonal and a closed-form solution from one matrix inverse; with item kNN it led a small-catalogue ranking table, though the cubic inverse limits it to catalogues of tens of thousands.
- [[EK-FAC Influence Functions for LLMs]] — EK-FAC approximates the inverse-curvature term in influence functions cheaply enough to trace a language model's outputs back to pretraining sequences at up to 52B parameters.
- [[Embedding Clustering and Anomaly Detection]] — Sentence-embedding matrices cluster and flag outliers far better than TF-IDF, with k-means, UMAP plus HDBSCAN plus c-TF-IDF, and kNN or LOF detectors, but each result needs its setup and a manual look.
- [[Encoder Tokens, Position and Attention Scaling]] — Self-attention plus mean pooling sees a set, so position must be added; a learned table and sinusoids tie, and the choice of what a token is (a single pixel, a row, a patch) matters more: patches matched or beat single pixels at a fraction of the time.
- [[End-to-End Memory Networks]] — A softmax-attention form of the Memory Network that stacks several read hops over embedded sentences and trains from question-answer pairs alone; on bAbI it came close to a supervised memory network and on language modeling it edged out LSTM baselines (self-reported).
- [[Entity-Disjoint Splits and Group Scores for Pair Matchers]] — Because match labels are transitive, a random pair split leaks the answer: close the labels into groups, hold out whole groups, block inside each side, and tune on group F1 when merging, since closure chains pair errors.
- [[Error Analysis by Confusion-Matrix Cell]] — Read a model's errors in order: name the positive class, set the table at the decision threshold, compare the four cells, read random rows before confident ones, then diagnose before touching the model.
- [[Error Analysis, Bias and Variance Gaps]] — Diagnose which gap to attack first: human level to train error (avoidable bias), train to train-dev (variance), train-dev to dev (data mismatch), dev to test (overfit to dev); count an error category before building a fix, since its share of mistakes is the ceiling on the gain.
- [[Eyeball and Blackbox Sets and Fixes That Fit the Reading]] — Reading errors overfits the rows read: keep a read set apart from a score set, fix classes not rows, count rounds, and expect even a well-argued mechanism fix to vanish on unread data.
- [[Feature Representation for Linear Models]] — Binning, interactions, polynomials, log transforms, selection and domain features give linear models shape; trees gain little from them.
- [[Feature Structures, Unification and SEM Features]] — Feature structures replace atomic grammar categories so agreement is shared across a production, and a SEM feature carries a meaning built during parsing, from SQL strings to logic with quantifier scope.
- [[Forecast Issue Time and Measured Leakage]] — Fix the issue time, horizon, information set and loss before fitting; every feature lag must be at least the horizon, and three common leaks were measured on one hourly series.
- [[Frozen vs Fine-Tuned Sentence Encoders]] — A pre-trained encoder is not one result: a small frozen encoder lost to TF-IDF at every label count, a larger one won with few labels, and fine-tuning DistilBERT first clearly beat word counts only at 5,000 labels (0.905 vs 0.886); measure on your task and keep TF-IDF as the bar.
- [[Global vs Per-Segment Forecast Models]] — Start with one global model plus the naive row, add the segment as a feature before splitting the data, and split only where later folds confirm it; one eight-fold table gave the wrong answer on synthetic cash-flow data.
- [[Gower Distance, PAM and k-prototypes]] — Gower handles missing values but equal weights give categories about three times the say of numbers; PAM needs an n-by-n matrix, and k-prototypes' gamma has no value that suits every world.
- [[Gradient Boosting Mechanics and Library Choice]] — Boosting fits shallow trees to loss gradients; the main libraries tie on accuracy within seed noise, so choose by speed, categorical and missing-value handling, and tune rounds by early stopping.
- [[Gradient Checking with Finite Differences]] — A centred finite difference gives a slow but independent estimate of every gradient; the relative difference to backprop separates a correct implementation (about 9e-11) from a planted 10% error (about 0.05), and the check is switched off in training.
- [[Gradient-Boosted Forecasting Relative to a Known Level]] — A regression tree cannot predict above anything it has seen, so divide target and lags by a level known at issue time; on a short single series this beat LSTM, TCN and DLinear, and quantile models give intervals whose coverage must be checked per fold.
- [[Group Fairness Criteria and the Impossibility Results]] — Demographic parity, equal opportunity, equalized odds, calibration and predictive parity ask different questions; with unequal base rates and an imperfect score they cannot all hold, so someone must choose and own the metric.
- [[Hard-Negative Mining and Measuring What It Fixes]] — Mining hard negatives did not raise recall on a tuned matching encoder but cut look-alikes in the top 10 from 0.494 to 0.363 on every seed; judge mining on what the negatives were meant to fix, keep in-batch negatives, and read a sample of what you mined for false negatives.
- [[HDBSCAN with KMeans Fallback]] — Clustering selection strategy that tries density-based HDBSCAN first and falls back to KMeans when silhouette drops below 0.25 — plus the diagnostic discipline that treats a fallback as a feature-quality signal rather than a resolved choice.
- [[HDBSCAN: Mutual Reachability and Stability Extraction]] — HDBSCAN replaces DBSCAN's single epsilon with a hierarchy over mutual reachability distance and picks flat clusters by total stability; McInnes and Healy speed it up with tree-based neighbour search and a dual-tree minimum spanning tree.
- [[Hyperparameter Search Budgets and Selection Optimism]] — Random, grid and TPE searches tie when the plateau is wide; the winner's score is optimistic by an amount set by how few rows chose it, so report on untouched rows or nested folds.
- [[ImageNet-A and ImageNet-O Natural Adversarial Examples]] — Hendrycks and colleagues keep only real images that a ResNet-50 gets wrong, giving test sets on which many unseen models fail; augmentation and extra data help little, architecture helps more.
- [[ImageNetV2 and the Adaptivity Gap in Test-Set Reuse]] — Recht and colleagues rebuilt the CIFAR-10 and ImageNet test sets by the original recipe: every model lost accuracy, but the ranking held and later models did not lose more, which points at distribution shift rather than overfitting to the reused test set.
- [[Imbalance Remedies Versus a Tuned Threshold]] — On a 3% default book no resampling remedy beat leaving the data alone and setting the threshold from the cost ratio, and every balancing method wrecked the probabilities until recalibrated.
- [[Implicit Feedback: iALS and BPR]] — With clicks and plays there is no negative signal and counts measure confidence, not strength; iALS weights every cell in closed form and BPR optimises pairwise order, and a published alpha of 40 was the worst setting on other data.
- [[Isolation Forest vs Gaussian Anomaly Detection]] — A per-feature Gaussian scores how likely a point is, an isolation forest how easy it is to cut off; pick by the shape of normal data, and irrelevant columns wreck the forest.
- [[Kernel SVM and the Kernel Trick]] — A kernel SVM is a linear model in an implicit richer feature space; C and gamma must be tuned together and features must be scaled.
- [[Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks]] — KANs put a learnable spline on every edge and only sum at the nodes; the authors report accuracy and interpretability gains on small science tasks at about 10x the training cost of an MLP.
- [[Label Bias, Proxies and the Cost of Mitigation]] — When the label is a past human decision every label-based metric inherits its bias and calibration can look clean; dropping the sensitive column leaves proxies, and mitigation costs depend on which label you price them with.
- [[Label Quality: Agreement, Noise and Confident Learning]] — Kappa measures agreement, not correctness, and is unstable on rare classes; label noise hurts probabilities more than ranking unless it depends on the row; confident-learning flags are a review list, not a cleaning rule.
- [[Latent Class Analysis for Mixed Data]] — Latent class models each group as a recipe of per-column distributions fit by EM; BIC can answer no groups, but correlated columns inside a class break local independence and inflate the class count.
- [[LDA, QDA and Naive Bayes Generative Classifiers]] — LDA, QDA and naive Bayes model each class's predictors and apply Bayes' theorem to get a posterior; which of them, logistic regression or KNN wins depends on whether the true boundary is linear, quadratic or irregular, and on class balance.
- [[Learning Curves and Bias-Variance Diagnosis]] — Plot train and validation error against training size to tell high bias (more data will not help) from high variance (it will).
- [[Learning From Corrections and the Audit Sample]] — A review queue only labels what the model chose to show, so silence is not confirmation and correction rate cannot separate a good model from a tired reviewer; measure with a random audit and retrain from audited labels.
- [[Learning to Rank: Pointwise, Pairwise, Listwise and LambdaMART]] — Learning to rank trains on one item, one pair or one whole list; NDCG is flat or discontinuous so LambdaMART defines gradients (RankNet's times |delta NDCG|) instead of a loss, yet on a matching task moving to pairs bought most of the gain and the NDCG factor little.
- [[Least-Angle Regression and the Lasso Path]] — Forward stepwise, least-angle regression (LAR) and the lasso are three ways to grow a sparse model one variable at a time; LAR gives the lasso path exactly (with a drop step), and pathwise coordinate descent computes the generalised lasso along a lambda grid.
- [[Lexical Diversity: Types, Tokens and Conditional Frequency Counts]] — Counting tokens against types, and counts split by genre or language with a conditional frequency distribution, with the normalisation steps that change the count and the caveats on raw counts.
- [[LIME Local Surrogate Explanations and Submodular Pick]] — LIME explains one prediction of any classifier by fitting a sparse linear model on weighted perturbations of that instance; SP-LIME picks a small set of instances whose explanations cover the important features.
- [[Linear Text Classifiers and the TF-IDF Baseline]] — TF-IDF plus a regularised linear model inside a pipeline is the text-classification baseline; Naive Bayes, trees and averaged word vectors compared, with coefficient reading as a leakage check.
- [[Lipton's Notions of Interpretability]] — Lipton argues interpretability has no single meaning and splits it into transparency (simulatability, decomposability, algorithmic transparency) and post-hoc explanation (text, visualisation, local explanation, example); on his conceptual argument linear models are not automatically more interpretable than deep nets.
- [[LSTM Forget-Gate Bias and Vanishing Gradients]] — Default-initialised LSTMs pass a gradient of about 10^-12 from the first step, no better than a plain RNN; setting the forget-gate bias to 1 or 2 (mind PyTorch's two bias vectors) restores long memory, and gradient clipping handles the opposite failure.
- [[Margin Classifiers, Hinge Loss and Kernel Smoothing]] — A support-vector classifier minimises hinge loss plus a ridge penalty and depends on the data only through inner products, so a kernel gives a nonlinear boundary at no extra cost; the same inner-product idea underlies string kernels and kernel smoothing.
- [[Match Errors Are Not Classification Errors]] — Score a matcher on whole lines, not pairs: 99.5% pair accuracy hid 27% wrong lines; a wrong link makes two errors, a one-to-one constraint hurt part payments, a subset-sum search fixes batches, and the margin over the second candidate beats raw score for abstaining.
- [[Matrix Completion by Iterated Low-Rank Fits]] — Matrix completion imputes missing entries from a low-rank principal-component fit to the observed ones; ISLP's Algorithm 12.1 does this on USArrests with M = 1 and recovers hidden values with a correlation of 0.63, short of the 0.79 an oracle gets from the complete data.
- [[Microsoft ML Workflow Survey: Nine Stages and an Activity Maturity Index]] — A Microsoft interview-and-survey study of software teams building ML describes a nine-stage workflow with feedback loops, finds data management the top challenge at every experience level, and proposes a six-dimension maturity index with no defined levels.
- [[Mixture-of-Transformers]] — A multimodal architecture that gives each modality its own non-embedding weights (feed-forward, attention projections, norms) with shared global attention and a fixed modality tag as the router; matches a dense Chameleon 7B with 55.8% of FLOPs (self-reported).
- [[ML Frame Sourcing — License, Horizon, Prevalence]] — Choosing public datasets for ML prototype frames on three constraints — a license that permits redistribution of derived artifacts, a time column that supports a temporal split, and a positive rate low enough to exercise the sufficiency guard — with the explicit note that class imbalance is not data insufficiency and that pre-registered hypotheses stay unedited after results.
- [[ML Stage Layering with Typed Contracts]] — An ML codebase organised by statistical technique (clustering/, regression/) is a function library with no seams, and every consumer of it writes one monolithic run.py; organise by process stage instead (ingest → transform → features → selection → training → evaluation → calibration → monitoring), give every stage boundary a Pydantic schema, and keep technique folders as libraries the stages call.
- [[N-gram Taggers, Backoff and Brill Transformation Rules]] — A part-of-speech tagger built from a default tag, suffix rules, unigram and bigram lookups chained by backoff, with Brill rules as a compact alternative; the held-out split decides which numbers mean anything.
- [[Named Entity Recognition and Sequence Tagging]] — NER is sequence labelling with BIO tags; a CRF learns tag-transition grammar, closed lists beat models via rules, and evaluation must be entity-level because O dominates tokens.
- [[Neural Text Models vs TF-IDF]] — On 5,000 labelled reviews a TF-IDF bigram logistic regression (0.886) beat averaged embeddings (0.847), a text CNN (0.741) and a BiLSTM (0.716), because from-scratch embeddings overfit; truncation and sequence-loss choices also move scores, and generation and masked losses have their own traps.
- [[Neural Turing Machine]] — A controller network coupled to a memory matrix through differentiable read and write heads, using content-based and location-based addressing, trained by gradient descent to learn copy, recall and sort; on small synthetic tasks it generalised to longer inputs where an LSTM did not (self-reported).
- [[Noisy Student Self-Training]] — Noisy Student trains a teacher on labeled images, pseudo-labels unlabeled images, then trains an equal-or-larger student on both with noise added, and iterates; the paper reports 88.4% ImageNet top-1 and large robustness gains.
- [[Notebook Dependency Staleness]] — Migration maps for the three library breaks that strand old ML notebooks — sklearn 0.20→1.4, TensorFlow 1.x→2.x, PyMC3→PyMC 5 — plus the two-phase triage that distinguishes a mechanical import swap from a genuine rewrite.
- [[NP Chunking with IOB Tags: Regexp, N-gram and Classifier Chunkers]] — Noun-phrase chunking turned into token tagging with IOB labels, so a tag-pattern grammar, an n-gram tagger and a feature-based classifier can be scored on the same CoNLL-2000 split.
- [[Object Detection Anchors, Non-Max Suppression and Focal Loss]] — Detectors predict a grid of anchor boxes, then filter by score and non-max suppression; one-stage detectors drown in easy background anchors, which focal loss (1-p_t)^gamma discounts, provided the classifier bias starts every anchor at 1% object probability.
- [[Offline Recurrence Sleep for Language Models]] — Before evicting old tokens from the KV cache, a hybrid state-space/attention model runs several offline recurrent passes over them to update its fast weights, so later answers need one forward pass; the benefit shows on synthetic reasoning tasks at small scale and is a latency shift, not a compute saving (self-reported).
- [[Ordered Boosting and Ordered Target Statistics]] — CatBoost's two permutation-based fixes for target leakage in boosting: categorical encodings and residuals are computed for each example only from examples earlier in a random order.
- [[PCA, NMF and Low-Rank Factorisation]] — PCA rotates to maximum-variance axes, NMF builds additive non-negative parts, collaborative filtering factorises a mostly missing matrix; scale before PCA.
- [[Penalised Linear Models: Ridge, Lasso and Elastic Net]] — L2 shrinks all weights, L1 zeros some, Elastic Net mixes them; alpha raises simplicity while classifier C works the opposite way.
- [[Per-Group Calibration Offsets by Partial Pooling]] — When groups share one score but differ in base rate, correct each group with one shrunken log-odds offset: a per-group threshold on few labels pays everything when none are positive, one global threshold ignores real differences.
- [[Pipelines Prevent Preprocessing Leakage]] — Any fitted preprocessing step must be refit inside each CV fold; a Pipeline does that, and feature selection outside the loop turns pure noise into a strong score.
- [[Platt Scaling for SVM Probabilistic Outputs]] — Platt scaling fits a two-parameter sigmoid P(y=1|f)=1/(1+exp(Af+B)) by maximum likelihood on held-out SVM outputs, with smoothed targets, so a sparse SVM yields calibrated posteriors; in 1999 tests it made fewer errors than a raw SVM on some tasks and was neither better nor worse than a regularized-likelihood machine overall (self-reported).
- [[Pre-Norm vs Post-Norm and Warm-Up]] — Post-norm keeps large output-layer gradients that need learning-rate warm-up (without it BLEU 8.45 vs about 34; at lr 0.01 accuracy stuck at 0.101), while pre-norm's gradients shrink with depth and train without warm-up.
- [[Prechelt's Stopping Criteria: GL, PQ and UP]] — Prechelt defines three classes of validation-based rule for stopping neural-network training, GL, PQ and UP, and compares 14 variants over 1296 runs; later-stopping rules bought roughly 4% lower test error for roughly four times the training time.
- [[Precision-Recall vs ROC and Average Precision]] — ROC AUC is a ranking probability unaffected by class balance, while PR curves and average precision track the flagged set on rare positives; the two can disagree and PR points must not be linearly interpolated.
- [[Probability Calibration and Reliability Curves]] — A score is calibrated when a 0.8 happens 80% of the time; check with a reliability diagram and Brier score, and fix on held-out data with isotonic, Platt or temperature scaling.
- [[Profile Then Select]] — Two-stage ML framing — a transform skill that profiles every column with stated thresholds and recommends without applying, then a select skill that refuses to pick a model family before the profile exists, treats "I don't know" as a first-class parked answer with a revisit trigger, and emits a draft (not confirmed) config when nobody is there to answer.
- [[Random Forests and Bagging]] — Averaging deep trees cuts variance only as far as their errors are decorrelated, which is why forests subsample features at each split; the out-of-bag score is a free validation estimate.
- [[Ranking Metrics as the Confusion Table Along a List]] — Precision and recall at k are the confusion table with k as the threshold; MRR and NDCG summarise across cut-offs and can disagree, so pick by whether one good answer or several are needed.
- [[Rare-Class Benchmark Quotas and Reweighting]] — A production-mix benchmark learns almost nothing about a rare class; equal quotas fix the spread but bias the headline unless each class's accuracy is reweighted back to its production share.
- [[Regularisation Knobs and Early Stopping]] — Every family has a complexity-limiting knob whose validation curve peaks in the middle; early stopping is the same penalty in training time, but libraries may keep the rounds waited past the best.
- [[Regularisation Under Noisy Labels]] — With 30% flipped labels a wide net memorises (train 1.000, clean test 0.694); early stopping on a clean validation set beat weight decay and dropout, and dropout should be off at test time and, for underfitting models, only used early.
- [[Resampling Before the Split Leaks]] — Resampling then cross-validating puts copies or interpolations of training rows into validation folds, turning a true 0.70 AUC into 0.98; keep the sampler inside the pipeline so it runs only on training folds.
- [[Residual Connections and the Degradation Problem]] — A block that outputs F(x)+x makes the identity the easy default, fixing the optimisation failure of very deep plain networks (62-layer plain net: train 0.379), and makes blocks optional so stochastic depth can drop them as regularisation.
- [[ResNeXt Cardinality in Residual Blocks]] — ResNeXt replaces each ResNet bottleneck with C parallel low-dimensional paths of the same shape, summed and implemented as grouped convolution; the paper reports that raising cardinality beats going deeper or wider at matched FLOPs on ImageNet-1K (self-reported).
- [[Review Route Thresholds From Priced Costs]] — Adding an ask-a-person route turns one threshold into two set by where three expected-cost lines cross; it beats a single threshold by a wide margin, but depends on the reviewer's cost and catch rate and on queue capacity.
- [[RMSE vs Top-N Ranking in Recommenders]] — A recommender is judged on the short list it shows, not on rating error; the model with the best RMSE lost to a popularity list on top-10, so score full-catalogue ranking, split by time, and put popularity and simple linear models in every table.
- [[RWKV Linear-Time Recurrent Language Model]] — RWKV replaces query-key attention with a channel-wise time-decay operator that runs in parallel for training and as a fixed-size-state recurrence for decoding; the paper reports parity with similar-sized transformers from 169M to 14B parameters.
- [[Sampled Ranking Metrics and MRR]] — Ranking each positive against 99 sampled negatives is a different metric from the full ranking, inflates scores several-fold and flipped the order of two models; rank the whole catalogue, and use MRR only when one good result ends the task.
- [[Selective Risk Guarantees: SGR and Conformal Sets]] — When a contract names an error rate instead of a price, a binomial-bound threshold search (SGR) keeps the promise but needs many labels; conformal sets are not a good cost router, yet class-conditional ones bound missed positives on any scorer.
- [[Semantic Analysis, WordNet and Lexical Ambiguity]] — WordNet organises senses into synsets with is-a and part-of links, Lesk disambiguates by definition overlap and fails without it, and logic-based semantics evaluates assertions.
- [[Sentence Embeddings as Features for Classical ML]] — A pretrained sentence encoder turns text into a cached matrix for ordinary scikit-learn heads; it beats TF-IDF most when labels are few, at about 50 times the encoding cost.
- [[Sentence-BERT Siamese Fine-Tuning]] — Sentence-BERT fine-tunes BERT or RoBERTa with pooling in a siamese network so sentence vectors can be compared by cosine similarity, avoiding the cross-encoder's pairwise cost at search time.
- [[Sentiment Analysis Methods and Failure Modes]] — Lexicons reach about 70 percent with no labels, TF-IDF bigrams plus a linear model about 90 percent, and sequence models overfit without more data; a maxlen mismatch at inference silently costs 11 points.
- [[Seq2seq Bottleneck and Attention]] — Encoding a whole input into one vector bottlenecks long inputs; letting the decoder weight all encoder states at each step fixed date normalisation (0.032 to 1.000 whole dates), and removing the recurrence from this idea gives self-attention.
- [[Sequence Models vs Hand-Built Event Windows]] — On generated card events with a planted order-dependent fraud pattern, boosting on velocity windows and recent events beat a GRU (PR-AUC 0.855 vs 0.791); order matters, but hand-written order features may capture it, and untuned baselines hand the GRU undeserved wins.
- [[SHAP Attributions and the Limits of Explanations]] — SHAP splits one prediction into a baseline plus per-feature log-odds that sum exactly; it explains the model, not the world, and studies show explanations help only for some tasks and people.
- [[SimCLRv2 Pretrain, Fine-Tune, Distill]] — SimCLRv2 pretrains a big ResNet without labels, fine-tunes it on 1% or 10% of ImageNet labels, then distils it into a same-size or smaller student using unlabeled images only.
- [[SimCSE Contrastive Sentence Embeddings]] — SimCSE fine-tunes BERT or RoBERTa with an in-batch contrastive loss, using two dropout masks of the same sentence as the unsupervised positive pair and NLI entailment/contradiction pairs when labels exist.
- [[Similar vs Interchangeable in Matching]] — Text similarity rewards both same-part and look-alike pairs, so a matcher's score should propose while extracted attributes decide (clash rejects, all read and agreeing accepts, unread goes to a person); the label chosen is itself a design decision.
- [[Slicing Errors With Correction and the Rate Identity]] — Slice error cost by nameable subgroups with Wilson intervals, correct the scan for many tests, confirm on unread rows, and expect group error-rate gaps because FPR, PPV and FNR are tied by an identity.
- [[SMOTE: Synthetic Minority Over-sampling]] — SMOTE adds synthetic minority examples interpolated between a minority point and its k nearest minority neighbours; Chawla et al. (2002) reported ROC-space gains, and later studies found no gain over a tuned threshold and distorted probabilities.
- [[Soft Inductive Bias, Benign Overfitting and Double Descent]] — Wilson argues that benign overfitting and double descent are not specific to neural networks: a flexible model with a soft preference for simple solutions, and compressibility-based bounds, account for both.
- [[Supervised Model Families and Complexity Knobs]] — Each classical supervised family has one knob that sets its complexity, a typical strength and a typical failure; sweep the knob first and start with a simple model.
- [[Support Vector Classifier, Slack Budget and Hinge Loss]] — A support vector classifier maximises a margin while a slack budget C lets some points fall inside it or across it; the same fit is a hinge loss with a ridge penalty, which explains why it behaves like logistic regression.
- [[t-SNE and UMAP Visualisation Rules]] — t-SNE preserves neighbourhoods for 2-D pictures only; read cluster shape not size or distance, vary perplexity, and never feed it to a model.
- [[Tabular ML Leakage Taxonomy]] — Six leakage classes for tabular ML — target, temporal, group, fit-before-split, resampling outside the fold, train/serve skew — all answering one question ("at the moment this line runs, what does it know that it would not know at prediction time?"), plus why a leakage verdict needs the person who knows how the data was collected, not a correlation ranking.
- [[Temperature Scaling for Post-Hoc Calibration]] — Temperature scaling divides a trained classifier's logits by one scalar T, fitted by negative log-likelihood on held-out data; it leaves predictions unchanged and cut ECE sharply on 2017 image benchmarks (self-reported).
- [[Test-Time Training with a Rotation Auxiliary Task]] — Test-Time Training fine-tunes a network's shared layers on one unlabeled test image with a rotation-prediction loss before classifying it; it lowered error under CIFAR-10-C corruptions without hurting the original distribution (self-reported).
- [[Text Classification Metrics: Macro-F1, Log-Loss and Calibration]] — Which metric fits which text task: macro-F1 exposes ignored small classes, log-loss punishes overconfident Naive Bayes, and multi-label needs micro versus macro averages and per-label thresholds.
- [[Text Preprocessing and the Stop-Word Negation Trap]] — Each text-cleaning step decides what a later model can see; default stop-word lists delete negations and flip sentiment, and the order of steps matters.
- [[Three-Level Duplicate Classification]] — A first-pass entity-resolution layer for multi-source ingestion that sorts candidate pairs into not_duplicate / possible_duplicate / confirmed_duplicate from deterministic signals that differ for time-bound and place-like entities, routes the uncertain middle to human review, hides confirmed duplicates through the existing visibility gate, and never deletes or merges automatically.
- [[Topic Models and Extractive Summarisation]] — LSI, LDA and NMF factor the document-term matrix into readable topics; choose k by coherence plus reading, check seed stability; document clustering and TextRank/LSA summaries share the same machinery.
- [[Train, Validation and Test Discipline]] — The test set is touched once; tuning happens on validation or CV folds, and every score is read against the majority-class base rate.
- [[Transfer and Multi-Task Learning Conditions]] — Transfer learning needs a much larger source task and shared low-level features, and can lose to training from scratch (frozen backbone 39.48% vs scratch 11.82% error); multi-task learning with masked loss uses partially labelled rows and halved error versus keeping only complete rows.
- [[Transformer Encoder Silent Shape Bugs]] — Three transformer-encoder bugs raise no error and still train: attending to padding (or an inverted mask), the batch_first=False default that makes digits attend across the batch (0.966 as a batch, 0.658 one at a time), and a (batch,1) vs (batch) broadcast in the loss.
- [[Tree Ensemble Interpretation Beyond Impurity Importance]] — Impurity importance rewards overfit noise; permutation importance on held-out data fixes that but is fooled by correlated columns, and partial dependence needs ICE lines and describes the model, not causes.
- [[Tree-Structured Parzen Estimator (TPE)]] — TPE models the density of settings that scored well and the density of those that scored badly, then proposes the setting with the highest good-over-bad ratio; Bergstra et al. compared it with a Gaussian-process search, random search and manual search on a 32-variable deep belief network space.
- [[Tuning an Embedding Model from Match Labels]] — A frozen pretrained encoder scored below a character n-gram baseline on code-heavy records; about 1,000 labelled pairs with in-batch softmax lifted recall@10 from 0.134 to 0.908, but a held-out product family dropped it to 0.715, so measure frozen, tuned and held-out against a character baseline.
- [[Two-Tower Retrieval with logQ Correction]] — A two-tower model embeds users and items separately so item vectors precompute into an ANN index and new items embed from features; in-batch softmax over-penalises popular items unless log sampling probability is subtracted in training only.
- [[UMAP Graph Construction and Layout]] — UMAP builds a weighted nearest-neighbour graph with a per-point connectivity offset, merges it with a fuzzy union, and lays it out by minimising edge-wise cross entropy; the paper reports it faster than t-SNE, with mixed accuracy results.
- [[Validating Clusters Without Labels]] — Internal indices, stability and classifier accuracy all look good on data with no groups, so compare every one against a shuffled-column control and validate on outcomes from outside the data.
- [[VGG Depth with Stacked 3x3 Filters]] — VGG fixes every other design choice, uses only 3x3 convolutions and varies depth from 11 to 19 weight layers; the paper reports error falling until 16 to 19 layers and a 6.8% top-5 test error for a two-model ensemble on ILSVRC-2014 (self-reported).
- [[Weight Initialisation and Activation Scale]] — Initial weight scale decides whether the signal survives the layers: too small or too large and a deep net never trains, while He (ReLU) and Glorot (tanh) scales keep activation spread stable; measure first-layer and last-layer activation std before blaming the optimiser.
- [[What Drives Sentiment: LIME, Per-Class Topics and Log-Odds]] — A polarity score says how many are unhappy, not why; LIME explains single predictions, NMF per class names the themes, and class log-odds needs a minimum count or a prior because rare words have high variance.
- [[Word Embeddings and Similarity Measures]] — word2vec, GloVe and FastText give dense vectors where shared contexts mean closeness; covers negative sampling, subwords, analogy and bias limits, document vectors, and why to normalise before cosine.
- [[Word Segmentation by Objective and Simulated Annealing]] — Segmenting unspaced text as a search: score a boundary bit string by lexicon size plus word count, then flip bits with a cooling schedule to find a low-scoring split.
- [[XGBoost System Design: Sparsity-Aware Splits, Quantile Sketch and Cache-Aware Blocks]] — Chen and Guestrin's XGBoost pairs a regularised second-order objective with sparsity-aware split finding, a weighted quantile sketch and a column-block layout for out-of-core and distributed training.

## Data Engineering

- [[Access Control Below the User, Audit Logs and Breach Scope]] — A control the subject can alter is no control: enforce access below the user, log who read what with purpose, and keep an inventory so breach scope is a lookup.
- [[Airflow 3 Cron Timetables and the Logical Date]] — From Airflow 3 a bare cron string means a trigger timetable whose logical date is the fire time, so ds names the day in progress; name CronDataIntervalTimetable to process the completed day.
- [[Atomic Commit and Why Two-Phase Commit Blocks]] — Two-phase commit gets all-or-nothing across nodes with a coordinator, but a participant that voted yes is stuck holding locks if the coordinator dies; it is acceptable inside one system and a poor fit across heterogeneous ones.
- [[Backfills, As-Of Dimensions and Past-Dependent Tables]] — With idempotent per-period tasks a backfill is a rerunnable, parallelisable loop; the hard parts are which dimension version old periods should use, tables that read the previous partition, late data restatement, and reconciling afterwards.
- [[Batched Rerunnable Data Backfills]] — Fill a new column in small keyed batches whose predicate selects only unfilled rows so a dead job simply restarts; a backfill alone is wrong on a live table, so a trigger for new writes and a repair pass come first.
- [[Blue-Green View Swap for Data Deploys]] — Build the new version of a table beside the old, audit it, then repoint a view in about a millisecond; rollback is the same swap while the old build is kept and nothing has been written on top.
- [[Breaking Schema Changes and Expand-Contract]] — Diff contract versions in the producer's CI to flag removals, renames, type changes and tightened constraints as breaking, and ship an unavoidable break as add-new, migrate consumers, then drop the old column in a new major version.
- [[Bridge Tables for Multivalued and Ragged Hierarchy Dimensions]] — A bridge table between fact and dimension lets plain SQL roll facts up a ragged hierarchy or share them among several dimension members, at the price of an overcounting risk.
- [[Business Dimensional Lifecycle and Dimensional Data Staging]] — Kimball's project road map for a dimensional warehouse, from readiness and requirements to deployment, with a staging design of a master cross-reference table, CRC change detection and surrogate-key replacement before load.
- [[Cache vs Source of Truth: Write-Through and Write-Behind]] — A system of record holds the authoritative data and derived data can be rebuilt from it; write-behind silently turns a cache into the source of truth for its flush window, and the test is to delete the cache and see what breaks.
- [[Change Data Capture and the Dual-Write Problem]] — Writing one change to two systems leaves them permanently different about half the time; CDC instead reads the database's own ordered change log, and a replay-safe consumer stores the log position per row and remembers deletes.
- [[Choosing a Store from the Access Pattern]] — Pick a data store by describing how records are found, how much one query touches, how data changes, its size and who operates it; row vs column is the first split, and each of ten store kinds is a bet that fits some patterns and hurts under others.
- [[Choosing Batch, Stream or Online Serving]] — Pick the serving shape by when the consumer needs the answer; batch is the default because a failure is a rerun, precompute works only when traffic concentrates on few keys, and online latency is mostly features and framework, not the model.
- [[Clickstream Session and Page-Event Fact Tables]] — Kimball models Web clickstream with a session-grain fact table for volume and a page-event table for detail, joined by a degenerate session ID, with page, event, session and referral dimensions.
- [[Concept-Drift Detector Families and Prequential Evaluation]] — A survey of online learning under drift sorts detectors into four families with different cost and precision, treats only real drift as needing adaptation, and scores streams prequentially because cross-validation breaks time order.
- [[Conformed Dimensions, the Bus Matrix and Drilling Across]] — Joining a finer-grain table or a second fact directly onto a fact multiplies rows and inflates sums with no error; share conformed dimensions, aggregate each fact to them first, then join.
- [[Connection Pooling Turns Failure into Waiting]] — Each Postgres connection is a server process under a fixed max_connections, so a burst beyond it fails; a pool shares a few connections and converts the failures into queueing, with PgBouncer transaction mode breaking session state.
- [[Consensus and Coordination Services]] — Consensus accepts a decision from a majority so it terminates while a majority is up; terms stop stale leaders, and most applications outsource it to a small coordination service or to a database row lock.
- [[Contract Change Impact Chain and the Same-Name Unit Change]] — Follow a column change from contract to catalog to metrics to named consumers using registration, lineage and the query log; a unit change under the same name passes every check.
- [[Copy-on-Write vs Merge-on-Read]] — Updating a lake table either rewrites whole files (copy-on-write) or appends delete and delta records merged later (merge-on-read); choose by update distribution, and compare Delta, Iceberg and Hudi as of their release snapshot.
- [[Core and Custom Schemas for Heterogeneous Products]] — When product lines have different facts and attributes, Kimball and Ross keep a core fact and product dimension for the common measures and a custom schema per line that reuses the core's keys.
- [[Dagster Assets vs Airflow Tasks]] — Dagster's nodes are tables that should exist and Airflow's are work to run, so Dagster can answer which partitions are missing while Airflow reports which runs happened; both leave the checked table already written.
- [[Data Catalog Metadata: Push, Pull and Measuring Adoption]] — A catalog needs both push and pull ingestion, separates tier from certification, and should be measured by stale descriptions and reads on certified tables, not coverage.
- [[Data Contracts Beyond Schemas]] — A data contract is a machine-checked agreement between producer and consumers covering structure, meaning, quality, freshness, ownership and grain; a units change such as dollars to cents passes every schema test and only a written semantic rule catches it.
- [[Data Engineering Foundations]] — The six stages of data engineering as a pipeline discipline — ingest, transform, orchestrate, warehouse, monitor, feature-serve — where each stage's job is to prepare data for the next, plus the modern-stack tools (DuckDB, Polars, Iceberg, Dagster) missing from Zoomcamp-era material.
- [[Data Incident Playbook: Contain Before Diagnosing]] — A data incident runs detect, triage, contain, repair, verify and postmortem in that order, because wrong data persists and spreads after the cause is fixed; contain first, never fix raw in place, and close only when the checks that fired are quiet.
- [[Data Mesh as an Ownership Model and Federated Governance]] — A mesh decides who owns each dataset, not where it is stored; it only works if global rules run as a gate that can fail a publish and name the owner.
- [[Data Mesh Topologies by Domain Granularity and Distribution]] — Strengholt describes six data mesh topologies that differ in domain granularity and in whether data moves peer to peer or through a central distribution layer, with the costs he sees in each.
- [[Data Quality Dimensions and Tests as Failing-Row Queries]] — Quality is six separate questions, each needing more context than the last; a test is a query that should return no rows, with a stated tolerance, and row-level tests are blind to anything that is only visible against time.
- [[Data SLOs, Error Budgets and Paging on the Symptom]] — An SLI measures, an SLO targets, and the error budget (one minus the target) is arithmetic for acceptable failure; page on symptoms that threaten the SLO, and adapt burn-rate thresholds to a batch cadence where one late run is a large share of the budget.
- [[Data Systems Foundations Reading Map]] — Map and rules of thumb for the storage-and-compute layer under data engineering: what a system promises, encoding and layout, concurrent writers, partitioning and replication, failure, consensus, distributed compute, durability and choosing a store.
- [[Data Vault Hubs, Links and Satellites]] — Data Vault splits each source entity into insert-only hubs (keys), links (relationships) and satellites (attribute history) so new sources cost a table each; the price is several joins per attribute, so readers get marts on top.
- [[dbt Build Test Semantics, Severity and Source Freshness]] — A dbt test is a query returning the rows that break a rule; in dbt build a failing error-severity test skips downstream models but the tested model is already rebuilt, and only freshness notices data that never arrived.
- [[dbt Incremental Models: Watermarks, Lookback and Strategies]] — An incremental model carries two code paths, and a watermark with no key duplicates the boundary row while a bare greater-than watermark loses late rows silently; use a unique key, a lookback sized from measured lateness, and a periodic full refresh.
- [[dbt Layers, Materialisations and the ref Graph]] — dbt compiles templated SELECTs into warehouse SQL and runs them in dependency order; staging, core and mart layers each read only the one before, so a wrong number traces to one line in one file.
- [[dbt Singular, Generic and Unit Tests with Reconciliation]] — Column tests cannot see a join that fans out; a reconciliation test of fact against source plus a unique test on every joined model's grain catches it, and unit tests with mocked rows guard logic that today's data does not exercise.
- [[dbt Snapshots: History as Samples]] — A snapshot is a Type 2 history built by comparing a mutable source with what it holds on each run, so it samples rather than records: changes between runs are lost and a missed run cannot be rebuilt.
- [[Degenerate, Junk and Role-Playing Dimensions]] — Three housekeeping dimensions in Kimball and Ross: a transaction number kept in the fact with no table, a junk table for low-cardinality flags, and one physical date table shown as several labelled views.
- [[Delivery Guarantees and Effectively-Once Processing]] — At-most-once, at-least-once and exactly-once differ only in whether position or output is saved first; effectively-once comes from idempotent keyed sinks, offsets stored atomically with the data, and idempotent producers, and it holds only within a boundary.
- [[Delta Lake Transaction Log]] — Delta Lake keeps a write-ahead log of table membership as files in the object store itself, giving ACID tables over Parquet data with no server holding table state.
- [[Dimensional Model Grain and the Four Design Steps]] — A star schema is agreements about what one row means, what can be added up and what a label meant on the day: declare the grain first, keep an Unknown row so nothing drops, and store the parts of any ratio.
- [[Dimensionality Reduction Plus Two-Sample Tests for Dataset-Shift Detection]] — Rabanser, Günnemann and Lipton compare ways of shrinking high-dimensional inputs before a two-sample test; testing a classifier's softmax outputs per dimension with a Bonferroni correction came out best in their image experiments.
- [[Distributed Compute Cost and the Single-Machine Threshold]] — Choose a cluster only when one query's input after pruning exceeds what a large single machine can hold; DuckDB and Polars spill to disk and push down filters, and scaling curves can hide large overheads.
- [[Distribution Monitors Need an Effect Size and a Fixed Reference]] — Only a distribution monitor sees a silent unit change; threshold on an effect size such as the KS statistic rather than a p-value, keep an approved fixed reference, and start with prediction drift when labels arrive late.
- [[Drift Tests: Significance, Magnitude and False Alarms]] — KS, chi-squared and PSI answer different questions: p-values go to zero on any shift at scale, PSI is noisy on small windows, and testing many features daily rings daily unless corrected; a domain classifier catches changes in feature relationships.
- [[Drift Triage: Broken Feeds, Benign Shifts and Concept Drift]] — Input drift can scream while error is flat, and concept drift can double the error while inputs and predictions look identical; triage an alert by checking the pipeline first, then measuring error on labels, and retrain only for real harm.
- [[DuckDB Embedded Analytical Database]] — DuckDB runs analytical SQL inside the host process using a vectorized interpreted engine, compressed column blocks and serializable MVCC, built from existing techniques rather than new ones.
- [[Encoding Compatibility and Schema Evolution]] — Data outlives code, so a format must be both backward and forward compatible; text formats silently destroy types, and schema-carrying binary formats (Avro, Protobuf) make evolution a checked property, with silent drift across files as the remaining trap.
- [[Erasure on Immutable Storage: Vacuum, Crypto-Shredding and the Ledger]] — Deleting one person from immutable files needs a file rewrite, a vacuum in table formats, a ledger replayed on restore or crypto-shredding, and a coverage check across every store.
- [[Error-Stream Detectors and Retraining Policy]] — When labels arrive, Page-Hinkley or CUSUM on the error stream trades detection delay against false alarms with no free setting; label delay decides whether this is possible, and scheduled retraining is the defensible default over triggers.
- [[Event Time, Watermarks and Late Data]] — Windows must be defined on event time, but a stream never says a window is complete; a watermark is a heuristic that fires windows, and allowed lateness trades lost late events against stale results.
- [[Factless Fact Tables and Coverage Tables for Non-Events]] — Kimball and Ross model an event with no measurements as a keys-only fact table, and model things that did not happen with a coverage table of every possible combination, queried by set difference.
- [[Faults, Load Parameters and Systems of Record]] — Design vocabulary for data systems: faults versus failures, why correlated software and human faults beat redundancy, choosing load parameters from the distribution not the mean, and separating systems of record from rebuildable derived data.
- [[Feature Stores: Parts and When You Need One]] — A feature store is a point-in-time join with a registry, an offline history store, an online latest-value store and a materialisation job around it; most teams with one model need only the join and a shared function.
- [[Fencing Tokens and Zombie Writers]] — A paused or deposed worker still believes it holds a lock; majority quorums settle truth inside a cluster, and a monotonically increasing fencing token checked at the resource stops stale writers, which is how zombie task attempts are neutralised.
- [[Four Levels of AI Design Complexity]] — A CHI 2020 framework says AI is hard to design for because of capability uncertainty and output complexity, and sorts systems into four levels by how many of the design challenges they bring; rule-based simulators prototype levels one and two but not three and four.
- [[Freshness Alerts Need a Date Spine]] — An age check on max(loaded_at) clears the moment a newer partition lands, with the hole still behind it; pairing it with a list of expected dates keeps firing until the gap is repaired.
- [[High-Watermark Incremental Extraction and Its Blind Spots]] — Reading rows newer than the last seen updated_at misses late-committed rows and every hard delete; an overlap window patches the first, nothing patches the second, and dlt's incremental cursor is the same mechanism.
- [[How Far Plain Postgres Stretches]] — A single relational server emulates key-value, document, search, time-series, graph and small-scale vector workloads at levels most applications never exceed; it stops being enough when writes exceed one primary, when scanning most of a huge table, for multi-region writes, search as a product, or blobs.
- [[Iceberg Metadata Tree and Hidden Partitioning]] — Iceberg stores a tree of metadata, manifest lists and manifests under an atomic catalog pointer, which enables planning-time pruning, hidden partitioning, partition evolution and schema tracking by field ID.
- [[Idempotent Backfills and Dated Corrections]] — A repair job that replaces one logical day per transaction is safe to run twice, corrections live in a dated table above untouched raw data, and a late partition needs a trigger: rebuild any mart row older than its input's newest load.
- [[Idempotent Batch Loads and Partition Overwrite]] — A task is idempotent when rerunning it for the same period leaves the same state as running it once; overwrite the period in one transaction by default, merge only where no period owns the row, and keep tasks pure and side effects keyed.
- [[Idempotent Retries and Request IDs]] — Because a timeout hides whether a write applied, retries are needed and duplicate effects follow; a request ID stored in the same transaction as the write makes a retry harmless, which is what most 'exactly-once' means.
- [[Infrastructure as Code Plans: Drift, Replacement and State]] — A plan loop of write config, plan, apply after approval exposes three traps: console edits show up as drift and get reverted, some edits force a destroy and re-create, and the state file must be stored remotely with locking.
- [[K-Anonymity, Quasi-Identifiers and Linkage Attacks]] — Removing names does not anonymise a table: combinations of quasi-identifiers single people out, k-anonymity measures it, and l-diversity covers the inference it misses.
- [[Kafka Ingest Model, Delivery Semantics and Stream Windows]] — Topics, partitions, keys, offsets and consumer groups define what ordering and replay a stream gives you; auto-commit is at-most-once, a schema registry turns silent schema change into a failed deploy, and four window types cover most stream aggregation.
- [[Kafka Log, Partitions and Offsets]] — A log keeps every record and lets each reader remember its own offset, which gives replay, multiple independent consumers, per-partition ordering and a lag metric that tells you whether a consumer is healthy.
- [[Lakehouse vs Warehouse Decision]] — A lakehouse puts a transactional metadata layer over open files to avoid the two-tier lake-plus-warehouse copy; what you gain is one copy and many engines, what you give up is data independence, multi-table transactions and some native performance.
- [[Lamport Timestamps and Total Order Broadcast]] — A counter-plus-node-ID gives a total order consistent with causality but only after the fact; total order broadcast, in practice a replicated log, fixes the order at delivery and so can implement uniqueness checks and compare-and-set.
- [[Lineage for Blast Radius and Rebuild Lists]] — Lineage is a graph of which job read and wrote which dataset; walking it downstream gives the blast radius of a bad table, upstream gives suspects, and joined to run history it gives the exact runs to redo.
- [[Linearizability, Ordering and Quorum Reads]] — Linearizability is a recency guarantee on one object, distinct from serializability; strict quorums with w + r > n do not deliver it, CAP is better read as consistent-or-available under partition, and causality gives only a partial order.
- [[Lock Queue and lock_timeout for Live DDL]] — A one-millisecond ALTER can stall every read because it queues for an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock behind a long reader while new queries queue behind it; a per-session lock_timeout with retry turns the stall into a short delay.
- [[MapReduce, the Shuffle and Spark Lineage]] — MapReduce splits a job into map, a hash-partitioned sort-and-copy shuffle and reduce, tolerating failure through immutable inputs and atomic output; Spark keeps the shape but recovers by recomputing partitions from lineage.
- [[Materialise or Query and Cost Tests]] — Decide whether to store a daily aggregate by comparing rebuild cost to scans saved at the real read rate, and stop cost regressions with dry-run byte budgets and per-job attribution instead of finding them on the invoice.
- [[Medallion Layers Need Tests]] — Bronze, silver and gold layers organise where problems get fixed but detect nothing; filters between layers drop rows silently unless a test counts them.
- [[Metric Definitions, Exposures and a Manifest Catalog]] — Define each metric once near the models so every tool asks for it, declare dashboards as exposures with owners to extend lineage, and start a catalog from the dbt manifest with a CI gate on missing owners.
- [[Minidimensions for Rapidly Changing Monster Dimensions]] — Kimball and Ross split the volatile or browsed attributes of a huge customer, account or service-line dimension into banded minidimensions keyed in the fact table, so the big dimension is not duplicated by every change.
- [[ML Deployment Challenges by Workflow Stage]] — A literature survey of deployment case studies maps issues onto four workflow stages plus cross-cutting concerns, argues that challenges appear at every stage, and ranks none of them.
- [[ML Deployment Challenges: Interview Study and the Three Vs]] — Shankar, Garcia, Hellerstein and co-authors interviewed 18 ML engineers and organise what operationalising ML involves around Velocity, Validation and Versioning, with reported practices on deployment stages, retraining cadence and alerts.
- [[ML Run Reproducibility Bar]] — A five-element bar that takes an ML prototype run from 'a finding in prose' to 'a finding a second person can refute from the record alone': tests in the default suite, a dry run on clean and planted-leak synthetic twins, guards the run owns, a validated run record with a reproduce check, and a data-handling sweep. It comes with the two ways a bar score misleads and the abort-vs-refuse guard taxonomy.
- [[ML System Design: Stages and Handoffs]] — Designing an ML system is a chain of stages from framing to correction loop, each handing the next a concrete artefact; the model is one row, the decision and its price come first, and the plain baseline doubles as the fallback.
- [[ML Test Score: Scoring Production Readiness by the Minimum]] — A 28-test rubric in four sections of seven (data, model, infrastructure, monitoring), half a point manual and one automated, scored by the minimum across sections so one strong area cannot hide a gap.
- [[MLOps Maturity Levels and CT vs CI/CD]] — Maturity models ask how much of the ML lifecycle runs without a human; level 4 (monitoring closes the retraining loop) is unsafe without strong monitoring, continuous training and CI/CD are separate loops, and a self-assessed level is a planning aid, not evidence.
- [[Model Registry Aliases and the Run Record]] — A tracker records every run with its data version; a registry names the runs that matter via numbered versions and movable aliases; the registry labels but does not deploy, so a service must resolve the alias and log the version it got.
- [[Model Rollout: Shadow, Canary and A/B]] — A model that won the offline gate has won on old data; shadow shows disagreement before labels exist, canary routes a stable hashed share, and A/B is the only option when predictions change future data.
- [[Monitoring Layers and What Each Can See]] — Operations, inputs, predictions and outcomes each catch a different failure; a drift score can fire loudly on a harmless input shift and read zero on an expensive new fraud type, so a random audit is what measures cost, and training on reviewed cases alone destroys automation.
- [[MVCC Row Versions and Snapshots]] — Multi-version concurrency control keeps old row versions so readers never block writers; a held-open snapshot pins versions and bloats tables, and table formats repeat the design at file scale.
- [[Object Store Guarantees and the Partial-Write Problem]] — An object store gives atomic single-key writes and little else: no rename, no multi-key atomicity, so a half-finished overwrite shows readers a wrong answer without error.
- [[One Big Table versus Star Schema]] — A pre-joined wide table reads somewhat faster and costs more storage, but a one-label change rewrites every affected row or file and a second grain has nowhere to go; treat it as a rebuildable cache of a star, never the source of truth.
- [[Orchestrator Core Loop, Logical Dates and Retries]] — Every orchestrator is a loop that orders tasks by dependency, skips what depends on a failure, retries with backoff and records runs against a logical date that is not the clock; scheduling is the set of expected runs minus the successful ones.
- [[Orchestrator Design Rules and Tool Choice]] — Keep domain logic in a plain package with orchestrator code as thin glue, hand off between tasks through storage, adopt an orchestrator on day 0, and pick the tool the organisation already runs unless starting fresh.
- [[Parallel Run and Reconcile at the Grain People Read]] — Moving a warehouse follows expand-migrate-contract at larger scale: run both side by side, compare per partition and key, because totals can match to the cent while no day matches.
- [[Parquet Row Groups, Pruning and Sort Order]] — How Parquet's footer statistics enable column pruning and predicate pushdown, why pushdown only works when physical sort order matches the filter, and how Parquet (compressed, at rest) differs from Arrow (uncompressed, in memory).
- [[Partition Pruning, Clustering and Column Pruning]] — Layout decides what an analytical query reads: named columns, partition filters and sort order each cut bytes scanned, and the savings multiply.
- [[Partitioning Strategies and Hot Spots]] — Range versus hash versus compound partition keys, why hot keys need salting, how secondary indexes fan out, and why rebalancing needs many fixed partitions rather than hash mod N.
- [[Passive Retraining and the Promotion Gate]] — Retrain on a schedule, score champion and challenger on the same holdout newer than both, and move the alias only on a win beyond a margin; the gate decides what proceeds to rollout, not what users get.
- [[Point-in-Time As-Of Join for Features]] — A feature row has a window it describes and a moment it became knowable; joining a training row to the latest feature known at its event time, not the same-hour window, keeps the offline metric honest even though it makes it look worse.
- [[Point-in-Time Recovery from Base Backup and WAL]] — A base backup plus every archived WAL segment recovers a database to a chosen moment just before a bad delete, on a new timeline, which a nightly dump and a replica cannot do.
- [[Pseudonymised Is Not Anonymised: Hashing Attacks and Keyed Pseudonyms]] — Hashing a low-entropy identifier is pseudonymisation at best; a keyed hash with the key outside the lake, scoped per domain, is the minimum.
- [[Re-embedding a Vector Store with a Second Index]] — A new embedding model is a schema change the database cannot see: queries still run and return garbage, so build a second collection, dual write, shadow test on retrieval, then swap an alias and keep the old one for rollback.
- [[Reconciliation Against a Producer Control Total]] — Nothing inside a table can show it is true; the cheapest accuracy test compares it with a second, independent account such as the producer's own daily row count, and it fails if that count is computed after a retry.
- [[Relational, Document and Graph Models and Schema on Read]] — Choosing a data model by the relationships the data has: documents suit self-contained trees, relational suits shared and many-to-many facts, graphs suit variable-length paths; plus schema-on-read versus schema-on-write and why declarative queries free the engine.
- [[Replica Lag and Replication Slots Retain WAL]] — Reads from a standby are stale even on an idle pair, long standby queries get cancelled by replay conflicts, an unread replication slot holds WAL until the disk fills, and capacity forecasts must include operations that need a table's size again.
- [[Replication Lag, Failover and Read Anomalies]] — Leader-based replication, why logical row logs power change data capture, how asynchronous replication loses acknowledged writes and reuses IDs on failover, and the read anomalies lag produces on replica-fed dashboards.
- [[Replication, Backups and the Durability Ladder Above the Disk]] — A replica survives the machine but copies your mistakes in milliseconds; async replication loses the lag at failover, sync replication stalls commits when the standby stalls, and only a restored backup with archived WAL survives a bad deploy.
- [[Restore Drill with an Invariant]] — Only a restore proves a backup: schedule the restore on separate hardware, check a data invariant rather than an exit code, record the time taken, and alert on missing success; a replica is not a backup.
- [[Reverse ETL Fingerprint Sync]] — Copy modelled data into a downstream tool by upserting on a stable ID, sending only records whose fingerprint changed, and recording each send only after it succeeds, so a crash costs nothing and a rerun sends only the rest.
- [[Rolling Out a Decision Router: Partial Shadow and an Audit-Sized Canary]] — A router that pays, reviews or rejects can shadow only where the champion produced outcomes, so it needs a canary with a stable unit, action limits and a clock set by label arrival, with an audit sized to the error jump it must detect.
- [[Row Stores vs Column Stores]] — OLTP and OLAP pull storage in opposite directions: row stores win on key lookups, column stores win on scans by about two orders of magnitude through pruning, compression, vectorisation and block skipping; includes rollups and the column-store write path.
- [[Safe DDL Recipes for Defaults, NOT NULL and Indexes]] — Split the slow proof from the strong lock: constant defaults avoid a rewrite, NOT NULL goes through NOT VALID then VALIDATE, and indexes use CONCURRENTLY, which leaves an INVALID index if it fails.
- [[Same-Weekday Median and MAD Volume Monitor]] — A row-count baseline built from the median and MAD of the last six values for the same weekday catches a 25% loss that a 28-day mean-and-three-sigma band misses almost every time; validate any threshold by injecting losses into real history.
- [[Scaling Per-Entity Model Tuning: SparkTrials versus Grouped Fits]] — Wilson's worked case of tuning one forecasting model per airport: distribute the trials of one model (SparkTrials) or distribute the entities (a grouped pandas_udf with a local Trials object), with the iteration-count rule for parallel TPE and how each choice changes MLflow logging.
- [[Semantic Layer: Metrics Compiled at the Requested Grain]] — A semantic layer stores metrics and join keys as data and compiles each request, refusing fan-out joins, recomputing ratios from parts and handling balances as semi-additive.
- [[Separated Storage and Compute and Warehouse Billing Shapes]] — Why modern warehouses split storage from compute, how the main engines differ in layout and billing, and why Redshift-style distribution keys decide join cost.
- [[Shuffle Skew and Salting]] — Joins and group-bys are shuffles; broadcast joins avoid moving the big side, and because hashing cannot split one key, a hot key makes one reducer the straggler, which salting plus a second aggregation stage reduces.
- [[Slim CI for dbt Models: state:modified and defer]] — Build only what a pull request changed by comparing the project with the production manifest, resolve unselected parents from production with defer, write to a per-PR schema, and make the test check required so a failing test stops the merge.
- [[Slowly Changing Dimensions Types 1, 2 and 3]] — Overwriting a dimension label rewrites published history; type 2 closes the old row and opens a new surrogate key so facts keep the label in force at event time, and a hash compare makes the load safe to re-run.
- [[Small Files, Prefix Layout and Storage Tiers]] — On an object store, file count and size decide request cost and whether column pruning works: partition by what you delete or backfill by, sort by what you filter on, and remember cold storage tiers lose money for tables that are read often or made of small objects.
- [[Spark Shuffles and When One Machine Is Enough]] — What a groupBy and a join cost in a distributed engine, which Spark habits to avoid, and when DuckDB or Polars on one machine is the better answer.
- [[Staging Dedupe Keys and Fact Join Row Loss]] — A dedupe is only as right as its key, and an inner join in a fact silently turns missing dimension rows into missing revenue with every test green; left join through an explicit unknown row and reconcile.
- [[Stream Joins Replay and Lambda Architecture]] — Stream joins depend on cross-stream event order, so a replay can silently change history unless the joined table is versioned; and a stream is worth its cost only if a faster number changes a decision, otherwise a micro-batch or one replayable stream path beats lambda.
- [[Table Format as a Commit Log over Parquet]] — A table format turns a bucket of immutable Parquet files into a table by keeping an ordered log of live files, committed by one atomic create, which gives atomic commits, time travel and optimistic concurrency.
- [[Testing an ML System Beyond Its Code]] — Code tests pass on a correct function fed broken data, so also test data validity, model behaviour (minimum functionality, invariance, direction) and model sanity (overfit a tiny batch), with injected seams and a container integration test against fake cloud services.
- [[Three Ways to Be Serializable]] — Serial execution, two-phase locking and serializable snapshot isolation each buy serializability at a different price: a throughput ceiling, blocking and deadlocks, or aborts under contention.
- [[Train-Serving Skew and the Shared Feature Function]] — Skew is the same input producing different features in two code paths; an unknown category vectorises to all zeros without an error, so the fix is one normalising feature function, a contract test, and logged serving features.
- [[Transaction Isolation Levels and Anomalies]] — What ACID letters actually promise and which anomaly each isolation level still allows: read skew, lost updates, write skew and phantoms, with the fixes (atomic update, row locks, constraints, serializable) and why level names differ across engines.
- [[Transaction, Periodic and Accumulating Snapshot Fact Tables]] — Kimball and Ross sort every fact table grain into transaction, periodic snapshot or accumulating snapshot; the choice decides whether rows are inserted or updated, how dense the table is and which facts can be summed over time.
- [[Unreliable Networks, Clocks and Process Pauses]] — A timeout means 'unknown', not 'failed'; clock skew of a few milliseconds makes last-write-wins drop acknowledged writes and inverts cause and effect; processes can pause unnoticed; checksums catch corruption that still parses.
- [[Vacuum, Bloat and Idle Transactions]] — Updates leave dead row versions that VACUUM only frees for reuse, VACUUM FULL returns space under an exclusive lock, and one forgotten idle transaction stops cleanup entirely; transaction-ID wraparound is the failure to know by name.
- [[Warehouse Cost Meters: Bytes, Slots and Egress]] — A warehouse bill is bytes scanned or slot-hours for compute, a small storage line and route-dependent egress; compute the on-demand versus capacity break-even before choosing, using list prices that carry a read date.
- [[Warehouse, Fabric and Lakehouse: Sizing the Physical Architecture]] — Modern data warehouse, data fabric and lakehouse differ in how many copies and governance layers they add; size the choice by measuring one query, and name the system of record.
- [[Write-Ahead Logs, fsync and the Durability Ladder]] — Saved is a ladder of rungs (process buffer, page cache, device, log, replica, backup), each surviving a different failure; fsync and a checksummed write-ahead log make a crash boring, and replace-by-rename keeps whole files atomic.
- [[Write-Audit-Publish and Atomic Visibility]] — Idempotency repairs a crash on rerun but not what readers see meanwhile; write beside and swap so readers never see half a write, audit the staged copy before publishing so a failed test protects the tested table too, and expose only what passes: an honest gap instead of a wrong number, and a containment tool during an incident.

## LLM

- [[Action Chunking]] — A vision-language-action policy infers at 1–10 Hz while robot control runs at 50–1000 Hz, so it emits 25–50 actions per inference and replays them while the next chunk computes — smoother motion bought with blindness to sudden change; plus the discrete-token vs. flow-matching action representations and the planner + VLA split.
- [[Adapting While Learning Tool Usage Adaptation]] — Adapting While Learning trains a model to answer directly after absorbing tool-derived knowledge, and to call scientific tools only on questions its own accuracy marks as hard; on custom science datasets an 8B model reports 59.31 against 30.20 for the base, with a tool-rate drop from about 91% to about 52% (self-reported).
- [[Agent Data Protocol (ADP)]] — A Pydantic schema that sits between raw agent-trajectory datasets and agent-specific SFT formats, turning dataset-times-harness conversion work into dataset-plus-harness; SFT on the mixed ADP data reports gains on coding, web and OS benchmarks (self-reported).
- [[Agent Fundamentals]] — Agent = LLM + Context + Tools, read as policy, observation space and action space — the five-part context and what breaks when each part is removed, the four-step tool call, why RL-trained 'model-as-agent' still needs a harness, the three places an agent can learn, and when a workflow beats an autonomous agent.
- [[Agent Lightning v1.0 Harnessed Agentic RL]] — Agent Lightning v1.0 trains agents behind a proxy that records token IDs per call, merges calls into sequences only when the exact token prefix survives, and computes advantage and loss per rollout; Qwen3.5-9B went from 41.8% to 56.4% on SWE-bench Verified (self-reported).
- [[Agent-FLAN Agent Tuning Data Design]] — Agent-FLAN tunes Llama2 for agent tasks by rewriting ReAct and JSON traces as chat, weighting data by capability and adding negative samples; it reports a 3.5-point overall gain over a re-implemented AgentTuning baseline and fewer hallucinated tool calls (self-reported).
- [[AgentArk Multi-Agent Distillation]] — AgentArk distils five-agent debate trajectories into a single student model by three routes (RSFT, DA, and PAD with a PRM and GRPO); the authors report about 4.8% average gain for a single agent, but most Qwen3-8B gains are under one point (self-reported, single runs).
- [[AgentGym-RL and ScalingInter-RL]] — A multi-turn RL framework with one HTTP server per environment, plus ScalingInter-RL, which raises the maximum interaction horizon in stages during training; the paper reports a 7B model matching larger models across five environments (self-reported).
- [[Agentic vs Agentive: Agent Model Position]] — A position paper separates agentic systems, whose competence sits in scaffolding and harnesses, from agentive ones that carry goals, identity and self-regulation inside the model, and proposes a Goal-Identity-Configurator architecture; it has no experiments, only theorems under stated assumptions.
- [[AgenticQwen Dual Data Flywheels]] — AgenticQwen trains small tool-use models with GRPO over three rounds on data from two loops, one that expands failed math problems and one that grows tool workflows into behaviour trees; the 8B model averaged 47.4 against 23.8 for its base (self-reported).
- [[AgentScaler Environment Scaling]] — AgentScaler builds function-calling environments automatically by treating every tool call as a read or write on a generated database, filters trajectories by final database state, and SFTs in two stages; its 30B model scored 70.4 on tau-bench Retail (self-reported).
- [[AgentSkiller Cross-Domain Trajectory Synthesis]] — An 18-step pipeline that synthesises tool-use environments (SQL databases, MCP servers, tool graphs) and cross-domain tasks verified by execution and an all-or-nothing rollout judge; SFT on about 11K trajectories reports strong tau2-bench results for a 14B model (self-reported).
- [[AgentTuning and AgentInstruct]] — Supervised fine-tuning of Llama-2-chat on 1,866 filtered GPT-4 agent trajectories (AgentInstruct) mixed 20/80 with ShareGPT; the paper reports large held-in gains and a held-out gain that is significant only at 70B (self-reported).
- [[AlphaCode Sample, Filter and Cluster]] — AlphaCode draws up to a million programs per problem from a fine-tuned encoder-decoder, discards those failing the example tests (over 99%), clusters survivors by outputs on generated inputs and submits at most 10; it reached top 54.3% on simulated Codeforces contests (self-reported).
